depending on the way the vertices in the perimeter of
\bar{W}
are subdivided.
Each of them gives a different selection
(P,C)
of pegs and corners of
W
.

We say that
W
is a
flat
r
-wall

of
G
if there is a separation
(X,Y)
of
G
and a choice
(P,C)

of pegs and corners for
W
such that:

•

V(W)\subseteq Y
,

•

P\subseteq X\cap Y\subseteq V(D(W))
, and

•

if
\Omega
is the cyclic ordering of the vertices
X\cap Y
as they appear in
D(W)
,
then there exists an
\Omega
-rendition
(\Gamma,\sigma,\pi)
of
G[Y]
.

We say that
W
is a
flat wall

of
G
if it is a flat
r
-wall for some odd integer
r\geq 3
.

Flatness pairs.

Given the above, we say that the choice of the 7-tuple
\mathfrak{R}=(X,Y,P,C,\Gamma,\sigma,\pi)

certifies that
W
is a flat wall of
G
.
We call the pair
(W,\mathfrak{R})
a
flatness pair
of
G
and define
the
height
of the pair
(W,\mathfrak{R})
to be the height of
W
.
We use the term
cell of

\mathfrak{R}
in order to refer to the cells of
\Gamma
.

We call the graph
G[Y]
the
\mathfrak{R}
-compass
of
W
in
G
,
denoted by
{\sf Compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
.
We can assume that
{\sf Compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
is connected, updating
\mathfrak{R}
by removing from
Y
the vertices of all the connected components of
{\sf Compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)

except for the one that contains
W
and including them in
X
(
\Gamma,\sigma,\pi
can also be easily modified according to the removal of the aforementioned vertices from
Y
).
We define the
flaps
of the wall
W
in
\mathfrak{R}
as

{\sf Flaps}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W):=\{\sigma(c)\mid c\in C(\Gamma)\}
.
Given a flap
F\in{\sf Flaps}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
, we define its
base

as
\partial F:=V(F)\cap\pi(N(\Gamma))
.
A cell
c
of
\mathfrak{R}
is
untidy
if
\pi(\tilde{c})
contains a vertex

x
of
{W}
such that two of the edges of
{W}
that are incident to
x
are edges of
\sigma(c)
. Notice that if
c
is untidy then
|\tilde{c}|=3
.
A cell
c
of
\mathfrak{R}
is
tidy
if it is not untidy.

Cell classification.

Given a cycle
C
of
{\sf Compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
, we say that

C
is
\mathfrak{R}
-normal
if it is
not
a subgraph of a flap
F\in{\sf Flaps}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
.
Given an
\mathfrak{R}
-normal cycle
C
of
{\sf Compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
,
we call a cell
c
of
\mathfrak{R}

C
-perimetric
if

\sigma(c)
contains some edge of
C
.
Notice that if
c
is
C
-perimetric, then
\pi(\tilde{c})
contains two points
p,q\in N(\Gamma)

such that
\pi(p)
and
\pi(q)
are vertices of
C
where one,
say
P_{c}^{\rm in}
, of the two
(\pi(p),\pi(q))
-subpaths of
C
is a subgraph of
\sigma(c)
and the other,
denoted by
P_{c}^{\rm out}
,
(\pi(p),\pi(q))
-subpath contains at most one internal vertex of
\sigma(c)
,
which should be the (unique) vertex
z
in
\partial\sigma(c)\setminus\{\pi(p),\pi(q)\}
.
We pick a
(p,q)
-arc
A_{c}
in
\hat{c}:={c}\cup\tilde{c}
such that
\pi^{-1}(z)\in A_{c}
if and only if
P_{c}^{\rm in}
contains
the vertex
z
as an internal vertex.

We consider the circle
K_{C}={\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}}\{A_{c}\mid\mbox{$c$ is a $C$-perimetric cell of $\mathfrak{R}$}\}

and we denote by
\Delta_{C}
the closed disk bounded by
K_{C}
that is contained in
\Delta
.
A cell
c
of
\mathfrak{R}
is called
C
-internal
if
c\subseteq\Delta_{C}

and is called
C
-external
if
\Delta_{C}\cap c=\emptyset
.
Notice that the cells of
\mathfrak{R}
are partitioned into
C
-internal,
C
-perimetric, and
C
-external cells.

Let
c
be a tidy
C
-perimetric cell of
\mathfrak{R}
where
|\tilde{c}|=3
. Notice that
c\setminus A_{c}
has two arcwise-connected components and one of them is an open disk
D_{c}
that is a subset of
\Delta_{C}
.
If the closure
\overline{D}_{c}
of
D_{c}
contains only two points of
\tilde{c}
then we call the cell
c

C
-marginal
.

Influence.

For every
\mathfrak{R}
-normal cycle
C
of
{\sf Compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
we define the set

{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}}(C)=\{\sigma(c)\mid\mbox{$c$ is a cell of $\mathfrak{R}$ that is not $C$-external}\}
.

A wall
W^{\prime}
of
{\sf Compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
is
\mathfrak{R}
-normal
if
D(W^{\prime})
is
\mathfrak{R}
-normal.
Notice that every wall of
W
(and hence every subwall of
W
) is an
\mathfrak{R}
-normal wall of
{\sf Compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
. We denote by
{\cal S}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
the set of all
\mathfrak{R}
-normal walls of
{\sf Compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
. Given a wall
W^{\prime}\in{\cal S}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
and a cell
c
of
\mathfrak{R}
,
we say that
c
is
W^{\prime}
-perimetric/internal/external/marginal
if
c
is
D(W^{\prime})
-perimetric/internal/external/marginal, respectively.
We also use
K_{W^{\prime}}
,
\Delta_{W^{\prime}}
,
{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W^{\prime})
as shortcuts
for
K_{D(W^{\prime})}
,
\Delta_{D(W^{\prime})}
, and
{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}}(D(W^{\prime}))
, respectively.

Regular flatness pairs.

We call a flatness pair
(W,\mathfrak{R})
of a graph
G

regular

if none of its cells is
W
-external,
W
-marginal, or untidy.

Tilts of flatness pairs.

Let
(W,\mathfrak{R})
and
(\tilde{W}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime})
be two flatness pairs of a graph
G
and let
W^{\prime}\in{\cal S}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
.
We assume that
\mathfrak{R}=(X,Y,P,C,\Gamma,\sigma,\pi)
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}=(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},P^{\prime},C^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
.
We say that
(\tilde{W}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime})
is a
W^{\prime}
-tilt
of
(W,\mathfrak{R})
if

•

\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}
does not have
\tilde{W}^{\prime}
-external cells,

•

\tilde{W}^{\prime}
is a tilt of
W^{\prime}
,

•

the set of
\tilde{W}^{\prime}
-internal cells of
\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}
is the same as the set of
W^{\prime}
-internal
cells of
\mathfrak{R}
and their images via
\sigma^{\prime}
and
{\sigma}
are also the same,

•

{\sf Compass}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}}(\tilde{W}^{\prime})
is a subgraph of
{\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}}{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W^{\prime})
, and

•

if
c
is a cell in
C(\Gamma^{\prime})\setminus C(\Gamma)
, then
|\tilde{c}|\leq 2
.

The next observation follows from the third item above and the fact that the cells corresponding to flaps containing a central vertex of
W^{\prime}
are all internal (recall that the height of a wall is always at least three).

{observation}

Let
(W,\mathfrak{R})
be a flatness pair of a graph
G
and
W^{\prime}\in{\cal S}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
.
For every
W^{\prime}
-tilt
(\tilde{W}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime})
of
(W,\mathfrak{R})
, the central vertices of
W^{\prime}
belong to the vertex set of
{\sf Compass}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}}(\tilde{W}^{\prime})
.

Also, given a regular flatness pair
(W,\mathfrak{R})
of a graph
G
and a
W^{\prime}\in{\cal S}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
,
for every
W^{\prime}
-tilt
(\tilde{W}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime})
of
(W,\mathfrak{R})
, by definition none of its cells is
\tilde{W}^{\prime}
-external,
\tilde{W}^{\prime}
-marginal, or untidy – thus,
(\tilde{W}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime})
is regular.
Therefore, regularity of a flatness pair is a property that its tilts “inherit”.

{observation}

If
(W,\mathfrak{R})
is a regular flatness pair, then for every
W^{\prime}\in{\cal S}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
, every
W^{\prime}
-tilt of
(W,\mathfrak{R})
is also regular.

Furthermore, we need the following propositions, that are the main results of
[
68
]
.

Proposition 5.2

(
[
68
]
)
.

There exists an algorithm that, given a graph
G
, a flatness pair
({W},\mathfrak{R})
of
G
, and a wall
W^{\prime}\in{\cal S}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
, outputs a
W^{\prime}
-tilt of
({W},\mathfrak{R})
in time
\mathcal{O}(n+m)
.

Proposition 5.3

(
[
68
]
)
.

Let
G
be a graph and
(W,\mathfrak{R})
be a flatness pair of
G
.
There is a regular flatness pair
(W^{*},\mathfrak{R}^{*})
of
G
, with the same height as
(W,\mathfrak{R})
, such that
{\sf Compass}_{\mathfrak{R}^{*}}(W^{*})\subseteq{\sf Compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
.

5.4
Canonical partitions

In this subsection, we define the notion of canonical partition of
a graph with respect to some wall. This refers to a partition of the vertex set of a graph in bags that follow the structure of a wall subgraph of the given graph.
For this reason, we start by defining the canonical partition of a wall, as a “canonical” way to partition the vertices of the wall in connected subsets that preserve the grid-like structure of the wall.

Canonical partition of a wall.

Let
r\geq 3
be an odd integer.
Let
W
be an
r
-wall and let
P_{1},\ldots,P_{r}
(resp.
L_{1},\ldots,L_{r}
) be its vertical (resp. horizontal) paths.
For every even (resp. odd)
i\in[2,r-1]
and every
j\in[2,r-1]
, we define
{A}^{(i,j)}
to be the subpath of
P_{i}
that starts from a vertex of
P_{i}\cap L_{j}
and finishes at a neighbor of a vertex in
L_{j+1}
(resp.
L_{j-1}
), such that
P_{i}\cap L_{j}\subseteq A^{(i,j)}
and
A^{(i,j)}
does not intersect
L_{j+1}
(resp.
L_{j-1}
).
Similarly, for every
i,j\in[2,r-1]
, we define
B^{(i,j)}
to be the subpath of
L_{j}
that starts from a vertex of
P_{i}\cap L_{j}
and finishes at a neighbor of a vertex in
P_{i-1}
, such that
P_{i}\cap L_{j}\subseteq B^{(i,j)}
and
B^{(i,j)}
does not intersect
P_{i-1}
.

Figure 3:
A
5
-wall and its canonical partition
{\cal Q}
. The red bag is the external bag
Q_{\rm ext}
.

For every
i,j\in[2,r-1]
, we denote by
Q^{(i,j)}
the graph
A^{(i,j)}\cup B^{(i,j)}
and by
{Q_{\rm ext}}
the graph
W\setminus\bigcup_{i,j\in[2,r-1]}V(Q_{i,j})
.
Now consider the collection
{\cal Q}=\{Q_{\rm ext}\}\cup\{Q_{i,j}\mid i,j\in[2,r-1]\}

and observe that the graphs in
{\cal Q}
are connected subgraphs of
W
and their vertex sets form a partition of
V(W)
.
We call
{\cal Q}
the
canonical partition
of
W
. Also, we call every
Q_{i,j},i,j\in[2,r-1]
an
internal bag
of
{\cal Q}
, while we refer to
Q_{\rm ext}
as the
external bag
of
{\cal Q}
. See
Figure 3
for an illustration of the notions defined above.
For every
i\in[(r-1)/2]
, we say that a set
Q\in{\cal Q}
is an
i
-internal bag of
{\cal Q}
if
V(Q)
does not contain any vertex of the first
i
layers of
W
.
Notice that the
1
-internal bags of
{\cal Q}
are the internal bags of
{\cal Q}
.

Canonical partition of a graph with respect to a wall.

Let
W
be a wall of a graph
G
.
Consider the canonical partition
{\cal Q}
of
W
.
The
enhancement
of the canonical partition
\mathcal{Q}
on
G
is the following operation.
We set
\tilde{\cal Q}:={\cal Q}

and, as long as there is a vertex
x\in G\setminus V({\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\bigcup}}}\tilde{\cal Q})
that is adjacent to a vertex of a graph
Q\in\tilde{\cal Q}
, we update
\tilde{\cal Q}:=\tilde{\cal Q}\setminus\{Q\}\cup\{\tilde{Q}\}
, where
\tilde{Q}=G[\{x\}\cup V(Q)]
.
We call the
\tilde{Q}\in\tilde{\cal Q}
that contains
Q_{\rm ext}
as a subgraph the
external bag
of
\tilde{\cal Q}
, and we denote it by
\tilde{Q}_{\rm ext}
, while we call
internal bags
of
\tilde{\cal Q}
all graphs in
\tilde{\cal Q}\setminus\{\tilde{Q}_{\rm ext}\}
.
Moreover, we enhance
\tilde{\cal Q}
by adding all vertices of
G\setminus\bigcup_{\tilde{Q}\in\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}}V(\tilde{Q})
in its external bag, i.e., by updating
\tilde{Q}_{\rm ext}:=G[V(\tilde{Q}_{\rm ext})\cup V(G)\setminus\bigcup_{\tilde{Q}\in\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}}V(\tilde{Q})]
.

We call such a partition
\tilde{\cal Q}
a
W
-canonical partition of
G
.

Notice that a
W
-canonical partition of
G
is not unique, since the sets in
{\cal Q}
can be “expanded” arbitrarily when introducing vertex
x
.

Let
W
be an
r
-wall of a graph
G
, for some odd integer
r\geq 3
and let

\tilde{\cal Q}
be a
W
-canonical partition of
G
.
For every
i\in[(r-1)/2]
, we say that a set

Q\in\tilde{\cal Q}
is an
i
-internal bag of
\tilde{\cal Q}

if it contains an
i
-internal bag of
{\cal Q}
as a subgraph.

We stress that given a graph
G
and an
r
-wall
W
of
G
for some odd integer
r\geq 3
,
a
W
-canonical partition
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
of
G
can have internal bags
that are adjacent to vertices of arbitrarily many other bags.
However, if
W
is a flat wall of
G
certified by some
7
-tuple
\mathfrak{R}
, the “flat structure” of the
\mathfrak{R}
-compass of
W
implies that every bag can be adjacent to only bags that contain vertices of the same brick of
W
.

The next result is also proved in
[
69
]
and
intuitively states that, given a flatness pair
(W,\mathfrak{R})
of “big enough” height and a
W
-canonical partition
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
of
G
, we can find a “packing” of subwalls of
W
that are inside some central part of
W
and such that the vertex set of every internal bag of
\tilde{\cal Q}
intersects the vertices of the flaps in the influence of at most one of these walls.
We will use this result in the case where the set
A^{\prime}
of
Proposition 3.7
is “small”, i.e., there are only “few” vertices in
A
that have “big enough” degree with respect to the central part of the canonical partition, and therefore
Proposition 3.7
cannot justify branching.
Following the latter condition and
Proposition 5.4
, we will be able to find a flatness pair with “few” apices so as to build irrelevant vertex arguments inside its compass.
In
[
69
]
,
(W,\mathfrak{R})
-canonical partition
is used to denote a
W
-canonical partition of
G
, where
(W,\mathfrak{R})
is a flatness pair of
G
.
However, in this subsection we provide a more general definition that does not take into account the flatness of
W
.

Proposition 5.4

(
[
69
]
)
.

There exists a function
f_{13}:{\mathbb{N}}^{3}\to{\mathbb{N}}
such that if
p,l\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 1}
,
r\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 3}
is an odd integer,
G
is a graph,
(W,\mathfrak{R})
is a flatness pair of
G
of height at least
\hyperref@@ii[@idealistic]{f_{\ref*{@idealistic}}}(l,r,p)
, and
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
is a
W
-canonical partition of
G
, then
there is a collection
\mathcal{W}=\{W^{1},\ldots,W^{l}\}
of
r
-subwalls of
W
such that

•

for every
i\in[l]
,
\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W^{i})
is a subgraph of
\bigcup\{Q\mid Q\text{ is a $p$-internal bag of }\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}\}
and

•

for every
i,j\in[l]
, with
i\neq j
, there is no internal bag of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
that contains vertices of both
V(\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W^{i}))
and
V(\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W^{j}))
.

Moreover,
\hyperref@@ii[@idealistic]{f_{\ref*{@idealistic}}}(l,r,p)=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{l}\cdot r+p)
and
\mathcal{W}
can be constructed in time
\mathcal{O}(n+m)
.

5.5
Bidimensionality of sets

In this subsection, we present the notion of bidimensionality of a set with respect to a wall of a graph.
This notion intuitively expresses the “spread” of a set
X
in a
W
-canonical partition of
G
. The crucial idea is that a set
X
of small bidimensionality cannot “destroy” a (flat) wall too much.

Bidimensionality.

Let
W
be a wall of a graph
G
,
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
be a
W
-canonical partition of
G
, and
X\subseteq V(G)
.
The
bidimensionality
of
X
in
G
with respect to
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
, denoted by
{\sf bid}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}}(X)
, is the number of internal bags of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
intersected by
X
.
The
bidimensionality
of
X
in
G
with respect to
W
, denoted by
{\sf bid}_{G,W}(X)
, is the maximum bidimensionality of
X
with respect to a
W
-canonical partition of
G
.

Subsection 5.5
and
Lemma 5.7
provide sets
X\subseteq V(G)
that can be used to apply
Proposition 3.7
to
\mathcal{F}
-M-Deletion
and
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
, respectively.

Every set
S\subseteq V(G)
of size at most
k
clearly
has bidimensionality at most
k
.

{observation}

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a finite collection of graphs, let
G
be a graph, let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and let
W
be a wall of
G
.
Then for every
k
-apex set
S
of
G
for
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
it holds that
{\sf bid}_{G,W}(S)\leq k
.

Moreover, given a
k
-elimination set
S
, we can find a set
X\supseteq S
of bidimensionality at most
k(k+1)/2
such that
G\setminus X\in{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
. To prove this, we first prove the following result, which
intuitively states that a
k
-elimination set can intersect at most
k
horizontal and vertical paths of a wall.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a finite collection of graphs.
Let
G
be a graph, let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
r,h
be odd integers with
r\geq h+k
, let
W
be an
r
-wall of
G
, and let
S
be a
k
-elimination set of
G
for
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
.
Then there is an
h
-subwall
W^{\prime}
of
W
with
V(W^{\prime})\cap S=\emptyset
.

Proof 5.6
.

Since
S
is a
k
-elimination set of
G
for
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
, there is an

\mathcal{F}
-elimination forest
(F,\chi,R)
of
G
of height
k
such that

\chi({\sf Int}(F,R))=S
.

We set
W_{0}:=W
.
For
i\in[k]
, we proceed to construct an
r_{i}
-subwall
W_{i}
of the
r_{i-1}
-wall
W_{i-1}
with
r_{i}\geq r-i

such that
for every
i\in[k]
there is a node
z_{i}
of
F
such that
(F_{z_{i}},z_{i})
has height at most
k-i

and
V(W_{i})\subseteq\chi(V(F_{z_{i}}))
.
This will imply the existence of a wall of size at least
r-k
whose vertex set will be a subset of
\chi(V(F_{z})
, where
z\in{\sf Leaf}(F,R)
.

Let
S_{i}:=V(W_{i-1})\cap S
.
If
S_{i}=\emptyset
, we set
W_{j}:=W_{i-1}
for
j\in[i,k]
.
Otherwise, let
u_{i}
be the least common ancestor of
\chi^{-1}(S_{i})
in
(F,R)
.
According to
Lemma 4.1
, since
W_{i-1}
is connected,
u_{i}
exists and belongs to
\chi^{-1}(S_{i})
.

We obtain an
(r_{i-1}-1)
-subwall
W_{i}
of
W_{i-1}
that does not contain
\chi(u_{i})
by taking the wall containing all horizontal and vertical paths of
W_{i-1}
aside from the ones intersecting
u_{i}
and we set
r_{i}:=r_{i-1}-1
(to simplify the argument, here we call the resulting graph a wall even when the height is even).
Note that since
W_{i}
is a subgraph of
G[\chi(V(F_{u_{i}}))]
that is connected, there is a
z_{i}\in{\sf Ch}_{F_{u_{i}},u_{i}}(u_{i})
such that

V(W_{i})\subseteq V(F_{z_{i}})
.
Notice that
(F_{u_{i}},\chi|_{V(F_{u_{i}}},u_{i})
is an
\mathcal{F}
-elimination forest of
G[\chi(F_{u_{i}})]
of height at most
k-i
.

Observe that
z_{k}
should be a leaf of
(F,R)
and therefore, since we have that

V(W_{k})\subseteq V(F_{z_{k}})\subseteq V(G)\setminus S
,

W_{k}
is a wall of
G\setminus S
of height at least
r-k\geq h
.

Now we prove our result regarding the bidimensionality of
k
-elimination sets.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a finite collection of graphs.
Let
G
be a graph, let
A\subseteq V(G)
, let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
r\geq 2k+3
be an odd integer, let
(W,\mathfrak{R})
be a flatness pair of
G\setminus A
, and let
S
be a
k
-elimination set of
G
for
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
.
There is a set
X\supseteq S
such that
G\setminus X\in{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
and
{\sf bid}_{G\setminus A,W}(X)\leq k(k+1)/2
.

Proof 5.8
.

Let
p={\sf odd}(r-k)
.
Let
W^{\prime}
be a
p
-subwall of
W
that is a wall of
G\setminus S
, which exists due to
Lemma 5.5
.
Let
C
be the connected component of
G\setminus S
that contains
W^{\prime}
.
Since
C\in{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
,
K_{s_{\mathcal{F}}}
is not a minor of
C
.
Moreover, since
S
is a
k
-elimination set of
G
for
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
, there is a set
P\subseteq S
of size at most
k
such that
(L,R):=(V(G)\setminus V(C),V(C)\cup P)
is a separation of
G
with
L\cap R=P
.

Let us show that
{\sf bid}_{G\setminus A,W}(V(G)\setminus V(C))\leq k(k+1)/2
.
Let
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
be a
W
-canonical partition of
G\setminus A
.
Let
l
be the number of internal bags of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
intersected by
P
and note that
l\leq k
.

Let
G^{\prime}
be the graph obtained from
G
after contracting each bag of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
to a vertex. It is easy to observe that
G^{\prime}
is isomorphic to a planar supergraph of an
h
-grid
H
, where
h=r-2
, together with an additional vertex that is adjacent to every vertex of the perimeter of
H
.

We let
[h]^{2}
be the vertex set of
H
, where
(i,j)
and
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
are adjacent if and only if
|i-i^{\prime}|+|j-j^{\prime}|=1
.
We will show that there is a separation
(L^{\prime},R^{\prime})
of
H
of order at most
l
that maximizes
\min\{|L^{\prime}|,|R^{\prime}|\}
.
Let
A=L^{\prime}\cap R^{\prime}
.
We suppose without loss of generality that
|L^{\prime}|\leq|R^{\prime}|
.
Notice that
l<h
. We take
A:=\{(i,j)\in[h]^{2}\mid i+j=l\}
, i.e.,
L^{\prime}
is the set of pairs of indices in the triangle bounded by
(0,0)
,
(0,l)
, and
(l,0)
. Thus,
|L^{\prime}|=l(l+1)/2
. It is easy to verify that this maximizes
|L^{\prime}|
.

Therefore, since the vertices of
H
are the internal bags of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
and
P
intersects
l
internal bags, it implies that one of
L
and
R
intersects at most
l(l+1)/2\leq k(k+1)/2
internal bags of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
.
Recall that
W^{\prime}
is a wall of
C
of height
p
.
It is easy to verify that an elementary
x
-wall
W^{*}
has
2x^{2}-2
vertices with
8x-10
vertices in the perimeter.
Hence, it has
2(x-2)^{2}
vertices not in the perimeter, and therefore the canonical partition of
W^{*}
has
(x-2)^{2}
internal bags.
Thus, the canonical partition of
W^{\prime}
has
(p-2)^{2}
internal bags.
Observe that each such a bag is contained in an internal bag of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
and therefore
V(C)
intersects at least
(p-2)^{2}
internal bags of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
.
Since
(p-2)^{2}\geq(r-k-2)^{2}\geq(k+1)^{2}>k(k+1)/2
, it holds that
{\sf bid}_{G\setminus A,W}(V(C)\cup P)>k(k+1)/2
.
Therefore,
{\sf bid}_{G\setminus A,W}(V(G)\setminus V(C))\leq k(k+1)/2
.

5.6
Homogeneous walls

In this subsection, we define homogeneous flat walls.
Intuitively, homogeneous flat walls are flat walls that allow the routing of the same set of (topological) minors in the augmented flaps (i.e., the flaps together with the apex set) “cropped” by each one of their bricks.
Such a flat wall can be detected in a big enough flat wall (
Proposition 3.5
) and this “homogeneity” property implies
that some central part of a big enough homogeneous wall can be declared irrelevant (
Proposition 3.6
).
The results presented in this subsection are from
[
69
,
70
]
.

Folios.

We say that
(M,T)
is a
tm
-pair
if
M
is a graph,
T\subseteq V(M)
, and all vertices in

V(M)\setminus T
have degree two. We denote by
{\sf diss}(M,T)
the graph obtained
from
M
by dissolving all vertices in
V(M)\setminus T
.
A
tm
-pair
of a graph
G
is a
tm
-pair
(M,T)
where
M
is a subgraph of
G
.
We call the vertices in
T

branch
vertices of
(M,T)
.
We need to deal with topological minors for the notion of homogeneity defined below, on which the statement of
[
9
, Theorem 5.2]
relies.
If
\textbf{M}=(M,B,\rho)\in{\cal B}
and
T\subseteq V(M)
with
B\subseteq T
, we call
(\textbf{M},T)
a
btm
-pair

and we define
{\sf diss}(\textbf{M},T)=({\sf diss}(M,T),B,\rho)
. Note that we do not permit dissolution of boundary vertices, as we consider all of them to be branch vertices. If
\textbf{G}=(G,B,\rho)
is a boundaried graph and
(M,T)
is a
tm
-pair of
G

where
B\subseteq T
, then we say that

(\textbf{M},T)
, where
\textbf{M}=(M,B,\rho)
, is a
btm
-pair
of
\textbf{G}=(G,B,\rho)
.
Let
\textbf{G}_{1},{\bf G}_{2}
be two boundaried graphs.
We say that
\textbf{G}_{1}
is a
topological minor

of
\textbf{G}_{2}
, denoted by
\textbf{G}_{1}{\preceq_{\sf tm}}\textbf{G}_{2}
, if

\textbf{G}_{2}
has a
btm
-pair
(\textbf{M},T)

such that
{\sf diss}(\textbf{M},T)
is isomorphic to
\textbf{G}_{1}
.
Given a
\textbf{G}\in{\cal B}
and a positive integer
\ell
, we define the
\ell
-folio
of
{\bf G}

as

{\ell}\mbox{\sf-folio}(\textbf{G})=\{\textbf{G}^{\prime}\in{\cal B}\mid\textbf{G}^{\prime}{\preceq_{\sf tm}}\textbf{G}\mbox{\penalty\ and $\textbf{G}^{\prime}$ has detail at most $\ell$}\}.

Augmented flaps.

Let
G
be a graph,
A
be a subset of
V(G)
of size
a
, and
(W,\mathfrak{R})
be a flatness pair of
G\setminus A
.
For each flap
F\in{\sf Flaps}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
we consider a labeling
\ell_{F}:\partial F\rightarrow\{1,2,3\}
such that
the set of labels assigned by
\ell_{F}
to
\partial F
is one of
\{1\}
,
\{1,2\}
,
\{1,2,3\}
.
Also, let
\tilde{a}\in[a]
.
For every set
\tilde{A}\in\binom{A}{\tilde{a}}
, we consider a bijection
\rho_{\tilde{A}}:\tilde{A}\to[\tilde{a}]
.
The labelings in
{\cal L}=\{\ell_{F}\mid F\in{\sf Flaps}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)\}
and the labelings in
\{\rho_{\tilde{A}}\mid\tilde{A}\in\binom{A}{\tilde{a}}\}
will be useful for defining a set of boundaried graphs that we will call augmented flaps.
We first need some more definitions.

Given a flap
F\in{\sf Flaps}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
, we define an ordering

\Omega(F)=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{q})
, with
q\leq 3
, of the vertices of
\partial{F}

so that

•

(x_{1},\ldots,x_{q})
is a counter-clockwise cyclic ordering of the vertices of
\partial F
as they appear in the corresponding cell of
C(\Gamma)
. Notice that this cyclic ordering is significant only when
|\partial F|=3
,
in the sense that
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
remains invariant under shifting, i.e.,
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
is the same as
(x_{2},x_{3},x_{1})
but not under inversion, i.e.,
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
is not the same as
(x_{3},x_{2},x_{1})
, and

•

for
i\in[q]
,
\ell_{F}(x_{i})=i
.

Notice that the second condition is necessary for completing the definition of the ordering
\Omega(F)
,
and this is the reason why we set up the labelings in
{\cal L}
.

For each set
\tilde{A}\in\binom{A}{\tilde{a}}
and each
F\in{\sf Flaps}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
with
t_{F}:=|\partial F|
,
we fix
\rho_{F}:\partial F\to[\tilde{a}+1,\tilde{a}+t_{F}]
such that

(\rho^{-1}_{F}(\tilde{a}+1),\ldots,\rho^{-1}_{F}(\tilde{a}+t_{F}))=\Omega(F)
.
Also, we define the boundaried graph

\textbf{F}^{\tilde{A}}:=(G[\tilde{A}\cup F],\tilde{A}\cup\partial F,\rho_{\tilde{A}}\cup\rho_{F})

and we denote by
F^{\tilde{A}}
the underlying graph of
\textbf{F}^{\tilde{A}}
. We call
\textbf{F}^{\tilde{A}}
an
\tilde{A}
-augmented flap
of the flatness pair
(W,\mathfrak{R})
of
G\setminus A

in
G
.

Palettes and homogeneity.

For each
\mathfrak{R}
-normal cycle
C
of
{\sf Compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
and each set
\tilde{A}\subseteq A
, we define
(\tilde{A},\ell)\mbox{\sf-palette}(C)=\{\ell\mbox{\sf-folio}({\bf F}^{\tilde{A}})\mid F\in{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}}(C)\}
.
Given a set
\tilde{A}\subseteq A
, we say that the flatness pair
(W,\mathfrak{R})
of
G\setminus A
is
\ell
-homogeneous with respect to
\tilde{A}
if every
internal
brick of
{W}
has the
same

(\tilde{A},\ell)
-palette
(seen as a cycle of
{\sf Compass}_{\mathfrak{R}}(W)
).
Also, given a collection
{\cal S}\subseteq 2^{A}
, we say that the flatness pair
(W,\mathfrak{R})
of
G\setminus A
is
\ell
-homogeneous
with respect to
{\cal S}

if it is
\ell
-homogeneous with respect to every
\tilde{A}\in{\cal S}
.

6
Vertex deletion to a minor-closed graph class

In this section, we prove our main result for the
\mathcal{F}
-M-Deletion
problem. The following theorem is a restatement of
Theorem 1.2
using the reformulation introduced in
Subsection 2.2
.

Theorem 6.1
.

For every finite collection of graphs
\mathcal{F}
, there exists an algorithm that, given a graph
G
and a non-negative integer
k
, runs in time
2^{k^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(1)}}\cdot n^{2}
and either outputs
a
k
-apex set of
G
for
{\sf exc}{(\mathcal{F})}

or reports that such a set does not exist.

This algorithm is a generalization of the algorithm in the apex-minor free case of
[
70
]
.
In order to give an algorithm without the apex-minor restriction,
we enhance the techniques of
[
70
]
with some new tricks.
We present this algorithm in this paper as a stepping stone to present the algorithms for elimination distance in
Section 8
and
Section 9
, since the techniques used are similar (however not the same).

6.1
Description of the algorithm for
\mathcal{F}
-M-Deletion

Our algorithm for
\mathcal{F}
-M-Deletion
has three steps.
In Step 1, either we can easily conclude with a positive or a negative answer or we find a big wall.
If we can find a large flat wall of bounded treewidth inside this wall, then we go to Step 2 and find an irrelevant vertex.
Otherwise, we proceed to Step 3 where, by using flow techniques, we find a set of vertices that intersects every solution, and we branch on this set or we report a negative answer.
The correctness of the algorithm is not trivial and will be justified in
Subsection 6.2
.
While the general scheme of the algorithm is similar to the algorithm in
[
70
]
for
\mathcal{F}
-M-Deletion
in the case where
\mathcal{F}
contains an apex-graph, here, in order to obtain a quadratic algorithm for general
\mathcal{F}
, we employ additional novel tricks so as to deal with the possible existence of many apices in all graphs in
\mathcal{F}
.

We define the following constants.

\displaystyle a=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@collaboration]{f_{\ref*{@collaboration}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}}+a_{\mathcal{F}}-1),

\displaystyle b=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@collaboration]{f_{\ref*{@collaboration}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}}),

\displaystyle q=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@categories]{f_{\ref*{@categories}}}(a_{\mathcal{F}},s_{\mathcal{F}},k),

\displaystyle p=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@provincial]{f_{\ref*{@provincial}}}(a_{\mathcal{F}},s_{\mathcal{F}},k),

\displaystyle l=

\displaystyle\ (q-1)\cdot(k+b),

\displaystyle r_{6}=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@differences]{f_{\ref*{@differences}}}(a+b,\ell_{\mathcal{F}},3,k)

\displaystyle d=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@deliberation]{f_{\ref*{@deliberation}}}(a+b,\ell_{\mathcal{F}})

\displaystyle r_{5}=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@philistines]{f_{\ref*{@philistines}}}(r_{6},a+b,a+b,d),

\displaystyle t=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@corollaries]{f_{\ref*{@corollaries}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}})\cdot r_{5},

\displaystyle r_{4}=

\displaystyle\ {\sf odd}(t+3),

\displaystyle r_{3}=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@idealistic]{f_{\ref*{@idealistic}}}(a_{\mathcal{F}},r_{4},1),

\displaystyle r_{2}=

\displaystyle\ {\sf odd}(2+\hyperref@@ii[@classifications]{f_{\ref*{@classifications}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}}+a_{\mathcal{F}}-1)\cdot r_{3}),

\displaystyle r_{2}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\ {\sf odd}(\max\{\hyperref@@ii[@unaffected]{f_{\ref*{@unaffected}}}(a_{\mathcal{F}},s_{\mathcal{F}},k),\hyperref@@ii[@idealistic]{f_{\ref*{@idealistic}}}(l+1,r_{2},p)\}),

\displaystyle r_{1}=

\displaystyle\ {\sf odd}(\hyperref@@ii[@classifications]{f_{\ref*{@classifications}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}})\cdot r_{2}^{\prime}+k).

Note that
r_{6}=\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k)
,
r_{5},r_{4},r_{3},r_{2},t=\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{c})
and
r_{2}^{\prime},r_{1}=\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{c+2})
where
c=\hyperref@@ii[@withdrawing]{f_{\ref*{@withdrawing}}}(a+b,a+b,d)
.
Recall from
Subsection 2.2
that we assume that
G
has
\mathcal{O}_{s_{\mathcal{F}}}(k\sqrt{\log k}\cdot n)
edges.

Step 1.

Run the algorithm
Find-Wall
from
Proposition 3.1
with input
(G,r_{1},k)

and, in time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(r_{1}^{2}+(k+r_{1})\log(k+r_{1}))}\cdot n=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2(c+2)})}\cdot n
,

•

either report a
no
-instance, or

•

conclude that
{\sf tw}(G)\leq\hyperref@@ii[@veneration]{f_{\ref*{@veneration}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}})\cdot r_{1}+k
and solve
\mathcal{F}
-M-Deletion
in time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}((r_{1}+k)\log(r_{1}+k))}\cdot n=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{c+2}\cdot\log k)}\cdot n
using the algorithm of
Proposition 3.2
, or

•

obtain an
r_{1}
-wall
W_{1}
of
G
.

If the output of
Proposition 3.1
is an
r_{1}
-wall
W_{1}
, consider all the
\binom{r_{1}}{r_{2}}^{2}=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{c}\log k)}

r_{2}
-subwalls of
W_{1}
.
For each one of them, say
W_{2}
, let
W_{2}^{*}
be the central
(r_{2}-2)
-subwall of
W_{2}
and let
D_{W_{2}}
be the graph obtained from
G
after removing the perimeter of
W_{2}
and taking the connected component containing
W_{2}^{*}
.
Run the algorithm
Grasped-or-Flat
of
Proposition 3.3
with input
(D_{W_{2}},r_{3},s_{\mathcal{F}}+a_{\mathcal{F}}-1,W_{2}^{*})
.
This can be done in time
\mathcal{O}_{s_{\mathcal{F}}}(k\sqrt{\log k}\cdot n)
.

If for some of these subwalls the result is
a set
A\subseteq V(D_{W_{2}})
with
|A|\leq a
and a flatness pair
(W_{3},\mathfrak{R}_{3})
of
D_{W_{2}}\setminus A
of height
r_{3}
then,
as in

, compute a
W_{3}
-canonical partition
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
of

and a collection
\mathcal{W}=\{W^{1},...,W^{a_{\mathcal{F}}}\}
of

-subwalls of

such that
for every
i\in[a_{\mathcal{F}}]
,

\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}_{3}}(W^{i})
is a subgraph of

and

, there is no internal bag of

that contains vertices of both

and

.
This can be done in time
\mathcal{O}_{s_{\mathcal{F}}}(k\sqrt{\log k}\cdot n)
.

For
i\in[a_{\mathcal{F}}]
,
let
W^{i*}
be the central
(r_{4}-2)
-subwall of
W^{i}
and let

be the graph obtained from

and the perimeter of

and taking the connected component containing

.
Run the algorithm
Clique-or-twFlat
of

with input

.
This takes time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(r_{5}^{2})}\cdot n=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2c})}\cdot n
.
If for one of these subwalls the result is a set
A^{\prime}
of size at most
b
and a regular flatness pair
(W_{5},\mathfrak{R}_{5})
of
D_{W^{i}}\setminus A^{\prime}
of height
r_{5}
whose
\mathfrak{R}_{5}
-compass has treewidth at most
t
, then we proceed to Step 2.

If, for every flatness pair
(W_{3},\mathfrak{R}_{3})
and for every

, the result is a report that
K_{s_{\mathcal{F}}}
is a minor of
D_{W^{i}}
, then we proceed to Step 3.

Step 2 (irrelevant vertex case).

We now obtain a 7-tuple
\mathfrak{R}_{5}^{\prime}
by adding all vertices of
G\setminus V({\sf Compass}_{\mathfrak{R}_{5}}(W_{5}))
to the set in the first coordinate of
\mathfrak{R}_{5}
, such that
(W_{5},\mathfrak{R}_{5}^{\prime})
is a regular flatness pair of

whose
\mathfrak{R}_{5}^{\prime}
-compass has treewidth at most
t
.
We apply the algorithm
Homogeneous
of
Proposition 3.5
with input
(r_{6},a+b,a+b,d,t,G,A\cup A^{\prime},W_{5},\mathfrak{R}_{5}^{\prime})
, which outputs, in time

, a flatness pair
(W_{6},\mathfrak{R}_{6})
of

of height

that is

-homogeneous with respect to

and is a

-tilt of

for some subwall

of

.
We apply the algorithm
Find-Irrelevant-Vertex
of

with input
(k,a+b,G,A\cup A^{\prime},W_{6},\mathfrak{R}_{6})
, which outputs, in time
\mathcal{O}(n+m)=\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k\sqrt{\log k}\cdot n)
, a vertex
v
such that
(G,k)
and
(G\setminus v,k)

are equivalent instances of
\mathcal{F}
-M-Deletion
.
Then the algorithm runs recursively on the equivalent instance
(G\setminus v,k)
.

Step 3 (branching case).

Consider all the
r_{2}^{\prime}
-subwalls of
W_{1}
, which are at most
\binom{r_{1}}{r_{2}^{\prime}}^{2}=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{c+2}\log k)}
many,
and for each of them, say
W_{2}^{\prime}
, compute its canonical partition
\mathcal{Q}
.
Then, contract each bag
Q
of
\mathcal{Q}
to a single vertex
v_{Q}
, and add a new vertex
v_{\rm all}
and make it adjacent to all
v_{Q}
’s. In the resulting graph
G^{\prime}
, for every vertex
y
of
G\setminus V(W_{2}^{\prime})
, check, using a flow augmentation algorithm
[
20
]
, whether there are
q
internally vertex-disjoint paths from
v_{\rm all}
to

in time
\mathcal{O}(q\cdot m)=\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{4}\sqrt{\log k}\cdot n)
.
Let
\tilde{A}
be the set of all such
y
’s.

<If
|\tilde{A}|<a_{\mathcal{F}}
, then report a
no
-instance.

If
a_{\mathcal{F}}\leq|\tilde{A}|\leq k+b
, then consider all the
\binom{|\tilde{A}|}{a_{\mathcal{F}}}=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(\log k)}
subsets of
\tilde{A}
of size
a_{\mathcal{F}}
.
For each one of them, say
A^{*}
, construct
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
by
enhancing
\mathcal{Q}
on
G\setminus A^{*}
.
Then, we distinguish two cases depending on whether for every
A^{*}
all its vertices are adjacent to vertices of
q

p
-internal bags of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
.

If each vertex of
A^{*}
is adjacent to vertices of
q

p
-internal bags of

\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
, then (due to
Proposition 3.7
)
A^{*}
should intersect every solution of
\mathcal{F}
-M-Deletion
for the instance
(G,k)
.
Therefore, the algorithm runs recursively on each instance
(G\setminus y,k-1)
for
y\in A^{*}
. If one of them is a
yes
-instance with
(k-1)
-apex set
S
of
G\setminus y
, then
(G,k)
is a
yes
-instance with
k
-apex set
S\cup\{y\}
of
G
.
If all of them are
no
-instances, then report a
no
-instance.
This concludes the case where each vertex of
A^{*}
is adjacent to vertices of
q

p
-internal bags of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
.

If for every subset
A^{*}
of
\tilde{A}
of size
a_{\mathcal{F}}
, there is a vertex of
A^{*}
that is not adjacent to vertices of
q

p
-internal bags of the given
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
, then report a
no
-instance.
This concludes the case that
a_{\mathcal{F}}\leq|\tilde{A}|\leq k+b
.

If for every wall,
|\tilde{A}|>k+b
, then report that
(G,k)
is a
no
-instance of
\mathcal{F}
-M-Deletion
.

Notice that Step 3, when applied, takes time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{c+2}\log k)}\cdot n^{2}
, because we apply the flow algorithms to each of the
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{c+2}\log k)}

r_{2}^{\prime}
-subwalls and for each vertex of
G
.
However, the search tree created by the branching technique has at most
a_{\mathcal{F}}
branches and depth at most
k
. So Step 3 cannot be applied more than
{a_{\mathcal{F}}}^{k}
times during the course of the algorithm.
Since Step 1 runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2(c+2)})}\cdot n
, Step 2 runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2c})}\cdot n
, and both may be applied at most
n
times, the claimed time complexity follows: the algorithm runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2(c+2)})}\cdot n^{2}
.

6.2
Correctness of the algorithm

Suppose first that
(G,k)
is a
yes
-instance and let
S
be a
k
-apex set of
G
.
The application of the algorithm
Find-Wall
of
Proposition 3.1
with input
(G,r_{1},k)
either returns a report that
{\sf tw}(G)\leq\hyperref@@ii[@veneration]{f_{\ref*{@veneration}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}})\cdot r_{1}+k
or returns an
r_{1}
-wall. In the first case, i.e., if
{\sf tw}(G)\leq\hyperref@@ii[@veneration]{f_{\ref*{@veneration}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}})\cdot r_{1}+k
, the application of the algorithm of
Proposition 3.2
correctly outputs a
k
-apex set of
G
.
We will focus on the latter case, i.e., where the algorithm
Find-Wall
returns an
r_{1}
-wall of
G
, say
W_{1}
.
Since
r_{1}\geq\hyperref@@ii[@classifications]{f_{\ref*{@classifications}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}})\cdot r_{2}^{\prime}+k
, there is an
(\hyperref@@ii[@classifications]{f_{\ref*{@classifications}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}})\cdot r_{2}^{\prime})
-subwall
of
W_{1}
, say
W_{1}^{*}
, that does not contain vertices of
S
.
Since
G\setminus S
does not contain
K_{s_{\mathcal{F}}}
as a minor, there is no
model of
K_{s_{\mathcal{F}}}
grasped by
W_{1}^{*}
and therefore,
due to
Proposition 3.3
with input
(G\setminus S,r_{2}^{\prime},s_{\mathcal{F}},W_{1}^{*})
,
we know that there is a set
B\subseteq V(G\setminus S)
, with
|B|\leq b
,
and a flatness pair
(W_{2}^{\prime},\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\prime})
of
G\setminus(S\cup B)
of height
r_{2}^{\prime}
such that
W_{2}^{\prime}
is a
W^{\prime\prime}
-tilt of some subwall
W^{\prime\prime}
of
W_{1}^{*}
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the canonical partition of
W_{2}^{\prime}
.
Let
G^{\prime}
be the graph obtained by contracting each bag
Q
of
\mathcal{Q}

to a single vertex
v_{Q}
, and adding a new vertex
v_{\rm all}
and making it adjacent to all
v_{Q}
’s.
Let
\tilde{A}
be the set of vertices
y
of
G\setminus V(W_{2}^{\prime})
such that there are
q
internally vertex-disjoint paths from
v_{\rm all}
to
y
in
G^{\prime}
.
We claim that
\tilde{A}\subseteq S\cup B
.
To show this, we first prove that, for every
y\notin S\cup B
, the maximum number of internally vertex-disjoint paths from
v_{\sf all}
to
y
in
G^{\prime}
is

k+b+4
.
Indeed,
if
y
is a vertex in the
\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\prime}
-compass of
W_{2}^{\prime}
,
there are at most
k+b
such paths that intersect the set
S\cup B
and
at most four paths that do not intersect
S\cup B
(in the graph
G^{\prime}\setminus(S\cup B)
)
due to the fact that
(W_{2}^{\prime},\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\prime})
is a flatness pair of
G\setminus(S\cup B)
.
If
y
is not a vertex in the
\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\prime}
-compass of
W_{2}^{\prime}
, then, since by the definition of flatness pairs the perimeter of
W_{2}^{\prime}
together with the set
S\cup B
separate
y
from the
\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\prime}
-compass of
W_{2}^{\prime}
,
every collection of internally vertex-disjoint paths from
v_{\rm all}
to
y
in
G^{\prime}
should intersect the set
\{v_{Q_{\rm ext}}\}\cup S\cup B
, where
Q_{\rm ext}
is the external bag of
\mathcal{Q}
. Therefore, in both cases, if
y\notin S\cup B
, the maximum number of internally vertex-disjoint paths from
v_{\sf all}
to
y
in
G^{\prime}
is

k+b+4
.
Since
k+b+4<q
, we have that
y\notin\tilde{A}
.
Hence,
\tilde{A}\subseteq S\cup B
and therefore
|\tilde{A}|\leq k+b
.
Hence, if
(G,k)
is a
yes
-instance we cannot have that
|\tilde{A}|>k+b
, so the algorithm correctly reports a
no
-instance at the end of Step 3.

Let
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
be a
W_{2}^{\prime}
-canonical partition of
G\setminus(S\cup B)
obtained by enhancing
\mathcal{Q}

on
G\setminus(S\cup B)
.
Let
\tilde{A}^{\prime}
be the set of vertices in
S\cup B
that are adjacent to vertices of at least
q

p
-internal bags of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
(recall that
\tilde{A}
is the set of vertices in
S\cup B
that are adjacent to vertices of at least
q
internal bags of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
).
Note that
\tilde{A}^{\prime}\subseteq\tilde{A}
and therefore
|\tilde{A}^{\prime}|\leq|\tilde{A}|
.

If
|\tilde{A}^{\prime}|<a_{\mathcal{F}}
,
then at most
a_{\mathcal{F}}-1
vertices of
S\cup B
are adjacent to vertices of at least
q

p
-internal bags of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
.
This means that the
p
-internal bags
of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
that contain vertices adjacent to some vertex of
(S\cup B)\setminus\tilde{A}^{\prime}
are at most
(q-1)\cdot(k+b)=l
.

Consider a family
\mathcal{W}=\{W^{1},\ldots,W^{l+1}\}
of
l+1

r_{2}
-subwalls of
W_{2}^{\prime}
such that for every
i\in[l+1]
,
\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\prime}}(W^{i})
is a subgraph of
\bigcup\{Q\mid Q\text{ is a $p$-internal bag of }\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}\}
and for every
i,j\in[l+1]
, with
i\neq j
, there is no internal bag of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
that contains vertices of both
V(\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\prime}}(W^{i}))
and
V(\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\prime}}(W^{j}))
. The existence of
\mathcal{W}
follows from
Proposition 5.4
and the fact that
r_{2}^{\prime}\geq\hyperref@@ii[@idealistic]{f_{\ref*{@idealistic}}}(l+1,r_{2},p)
.

The fact that the
p
-internal bags
of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
that contain vertices adjacent to some vertex of
(S\cup B)\setminus\tilde{A}^{\prime}
are at most
l
implies that
there exists an
i\in[l+1]
such that
no vertex of
V(\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\prime}}({W^{i}}))
is adjacent, in
G
, to a vertex in
(S\cup B)\setminus\tilde{A}^{\prime}
.
Let
W_{2}:=W^{i}
, let
W_{2}^{*}
be the central
(r_{2}-2)
-subwall of
W_{2}
, and let
D_{W_{2}}
be the graph obtained from
G
by removing the perimeter of
W_{2}
and taking the connected component that contains
W_{2}^{*}
.
Since no vertex of
V(\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\prime}}({W^{i}}))
is adjacent, in
G
, to a vertex in
(S\cup B)\setminus\tilde{A}^{\prime}
,
any path in
D_{W_{2}}
going from a vertex of
W_{2}^{*}
to a vertex in
S
must intersect a vertex of
\tilde{A}^{\prime}
.
Thus, there is no model of
K_{s_{\mathcal{F}}+a_{\mathcal{F}}-1}
grasped by
W_{2}^{*}
in
D_{W_{2}}
, because otherwise,
K_{s_{\mathcal{F}}}
would be a minor of
G\setminus S
.
So, by applying the algorithm
Grasped-or-Flat
of
Proposition 3.3
with input
(D_{W_{2}},r_{3},s_{\mathcal{F}}+a_{\mathcal{F}}-1,W_{2}^{*})
, since
r_{2}-2\geq\hyperref@@ii[@classifications]{f_{\ref*{@classifications}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}}+a_{\mathcal{F}}-1)\cdot r_{3}
, we should find a set
A\subseteq V(D_{W_{2}})
with
|A|\leq a
and a flatness pair
(W_{3},\mathfrak{R}_{3})
of
D_{W_{2}}\setminus A
of height
r_{3}
, such that
W_{3}
is a tilt of some subwall
\tilde{W}_{3}
of
W_{2}
.

Let
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
be a
W_{3}
-canonical partition of
D_{W_{2}}\setminus A
.
Let
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}=\{W^{1},...,W^{a_{\mathcal{F}}}\}
be a collection of
r_{4}
-subwalls of
W_{3}
such that
for every
i\in[a_{\mathcal{F}}]
,

\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}_{3}}(W^{i})
is a subgraph of
\bigcup\{Q\mid Q\text{ is an internal bag of }\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}\}
and
for every
i,j\in[a_{\mathcal{F}}]
, with
i\neq j
, there is no internal bag of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
that contains vertices of both
V(\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}_{3}}(W^{i}))
and
V(\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}_{3}}(W^{j}))
.
Since
|\tilde{A}^{\prime}|<a_{\mathcal{F}}
, there is an
i\in[a_{\mathcal{F}}]
such that
V(\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}_{3}}(W^{i}))
does not intersect
\tilde{A}^{\prime}
.
The existence of
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
follows from
Proposition 5.4
and the fact that
r_{3}\geq\hyperref@@ii[@idealistic]{f_{\ref*{@idealistic}}}(a_{\mathcal{F}},r_{4},1)
.

Let
W_{4}:=W^{i}
.
Let
W_{4}^{*}
be the central
(r_{4}-2)
-subwall of
W_{4}
and let
D_{W_{4}}
be the graph obtained from
D_{W_{2}}
after removing
A
and the perimeter of
W_{4}
and taking the connected component containing
W_{4}^{*}
.
Observe that any path between a vertex of
S
and a vertex of
V(\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}_{3}}(W_{4}))
in
D_{W_{2}}
intersects
\tilde{A}^{\prime}
.
Since
\tilde{A}^{\prime}
does not intersect
V(\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}_{3}}(W_{4}))
, it implies that
\tilde{A}^{\prime}
does not intersect
D_{W_{4}}
, and thus
S\cap D_{W_{4}}=\emptyset
.
Therefore,
D_{W_{4}}
is a minor of
G\setminus S
and
K_{s_{\mathcal{F}}}
is not a minor of
D_{W_{4}}
.
Moreover,
W_{4}^{*}
is a wall of
D_{W_{4}}
of height
r_{4}-2\geq t+1
, so
{\sf tw}(D_{W_{4}})>t=\hyperref@@ii[@corollaries]{f_{\ref*{@corollaries}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}})\cdot r_{5}
.
Therefore, by applying the algorithm
Clique-or-twFlat
of
Proposition 3.4
with input
(D_{W_{4}},r_{5},s_{\mathcal{F}})
, we should obtain a set
A^{\prime}
of size at most
b
and a regular flatness pair
(W_{5},\mathfrak{R}_{5})
of
D_{W_{4}}\setminus A^{\prime}
of height
r_{5}
whose
\mathfrak{R}_{5}
-compass has treewidth at most
t
.
All this is checked in Step 1, and thus, the algorithm should run Step 2.

If
|\tilde{A}^{\prime}|\geq a_{\mathcal{F}}
, then, due to
Proposition 3.7
and the fact that
r_{2}^{\prime}\geq\hyperref@@ii[@unaffected]{f_{\ref*{@unaffected}}}(a_{\mathcal{F}},s_{\mathcal{F}},k)
, for any set
X\subseteq V(G)
such that
{\sf bid}_{G\setminus(S\cup B),W_{2}^{\prime}}(X)\leq k
and such that
G\setminus X\in{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
, it holds that
X\cap\tilde{A}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
. In particular, for any
k
-apex set
S^{\prime}
,
S^{\prime}\cap\tilde{A}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
due to
Subsection 5.5
.
Thus, there is a vertex
y\in\tilde{A}^{\prime}
such that
(G\setminus y,k-1)
is a
yes
-instance.
Hence, if the algorithm runs Step 3, it finds a vertex
y\in\tilde{A}^{\prime}
such that
(G\setminus y,k-1)
is a
yes
-instance.

Note that the enhancement
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
of the canonical partition
\mathcal{Q}
is not unique. In particular,
\tilde{A}^{\prime}
depends on
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
.
However, as long as there is such a
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
such that
|\tilde{A}^{\prime}|<a_{\mathcal{F}}
, the algorithm finds the wanted flatness pair
(W_{4},\mathfrak{R}_{4})
in Step 1 and then runs Step 2.
Hence, if
(G,k)
is a
yes
-instance, the algorithm runs Step 3 only if for all such
\tilde{A}^{\prime}
,
|\tilde{A}^{\prime}|\geq a_{\mathcal{F}}
. Note that, since
|\tilde{A}|\geq|\tilde{A}^{\prime}|
, in this case we have that, for all such
\tilde{A}^{\prime}
,
|\tilde{A}|\geq a_{\mathcal{F}}
.
This justifies the arbitrary canonical partition enhancement in Step 3 and the fact
that, if
|\tilde{A}|<a_{\mathcal{F}}
in Step 3, then the algorithm reports a
no
-instance.

Let us now show the correctness of Step 2, and for this we do not suppose anymore that
(G,k)
is a
yes
-instance since the argument is the same for both types of instances.
Suppose that the algorithm finds in Step 1 a set
A^{\prime}
of size at most
b
and a regular flatness pair
(W_{5},\mathfrak{R}_{5})
of
D_{W_{4}}\setminus A^{\prime}
of height
r_{5}
whose
\mathfrak{R}_{5}
-compass has treewidth at most
t
.
We obtain a 7-tuple
\mathfrak{R}_{5}^{\prime}
by adding all vertices of
G\setminus V({\sf Compass}_{\mathfrak{R}_{5}}(W_{5}))
to the set in the first coordinate of
\mathfrak{R}_{5}
. Since
(W_{5},\mathfrak{R}_{5})
is a regular flatness pair of
D_{W_{4}}\setminus A^{\prime}
and since the vertices added in
\mathfrak{R}_{5}^{\prime}
are either in
A
, or adjacent at most to the perimeter of
W_{4}
, then
(W_{5},\mathfrak{R}_{5}^{\prime})
is a regular flatness pair of
G\setminus(A\cup A^{\prime})
. Since
{\sf Compass}_{\mathfrak{R}_{5}}(W_{5})={\sf Compass}_{\mathfrak{R}_{5}^{\prime}}(W_{5})
,
{\sf Compass}_{\mathfrak{R}_{5}^{\prime}}(W_{5})
has treewidth at most
t
.
Thus, if we apply the algorithm
Homogeneous
of
Proposition 3.5
with input
(r_{6},a+b,a+b,d,t,G,A\cup A^{\prime},W_{5},\mathfrak{R}_{5}^{\prime})
we obtain a flatness pair
(W_{6},\mathfrak{R}_{6})
of
G\setminus(A\cup A^{\prime})
of height
r_{6}
that is
d
-homogeneous with respect to
2^{A\cup A^{\prime}}
and is a
W^{*}
-tilt of
(W_{5},\mathfrak{R}_{5}^{\prime})
for some subwall
W^{*}
of
W_{5}
.
Due to
Subsection 5.3
, we know that
(W_{6},\mathfrak{R}_{6})
is regular.
Since
|A\cup A^{\prime}|\leq a+b
, for any set
X\subseteq V(G)
,
|A\setminus X|\leq a+b
.
Since
G\setminus S\in{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
and
Subsection 5.5
implies that
{\sf bid}_{G\setminus(A\cup A^{\prime}),W_{6}}(S)\leq k
,
by applying the algorithm
Find-Irrelevant Vertex
of
Proposition 3.6
with input
(k,a+b,G,A\cup A^{\prime},W_{6},\mathfrak{R}_{6})
, we obtain a vertex
v
such that
G\setminus S\in{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
if and only if
G\setminus(S\setminus v)\in{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
.
It follows that
(G,k)
and
(G\setminus v,k)
are indeed equivalent instances of
\mathcal{F}
-M-Deletion
.

We now suppose that
(G,k)
is a
no
-instance.
In the beginning of Step 1, the algorithm either reports a
no
-instance or finds a wall.
In the latter case, the algorithm either goes to Step 2 or Step 3.
If it runs Step 2, the previous paragraph justifies that the algorithm finds a vertex
v
such that
(G\setminus v,k)
is a
no
-instance.
If the algorithm runs Step 3, then it either reports a
no
-instance or recursively runs on instances
(G\setminus y,k-1)
.
If
(G\setminus y,k-1)
is
yes
-instance, then so is
(G,k)
. Thus,
(G\setminus y,k-1)
is a
no
-instance for every considered vertex
y
and the algorithm always reports a
no
-instance.
Hence,
Theorem 6.1
follows.

7
Solving
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
on tree decompositions

In the rest of the paper (except for
Section 11
) we focus on
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
.
In order to design an algorithm for this problem and prove
Theorem 1.2
and
Theorem 1.3

, we follow the same scheme as for
\mathcal{F}
-M-Deletion
.
The first step of this strategy is to present a dynamic programming algorithm that will allow us to solve
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
for instances of bounded treewidth in
FPT
-time, namely
Theorem 1.4
.
This is the analogue of for
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
of the corresponding result for
\mathcal{F}
-M-Deletion
, namely
Proposition 3.2
. The following theorem is a reformulation of
Theorem 1.4
.

Theorem 7.1
.

For every finite collection of graphs
\mathcal{F}
, there exists an algorithm that, given a graph
G
of treewidth at most
{\sf tw}
and a non-negative integer
k
, decides whether
{\sf ed}_{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}(G)\leq k
in time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}({\sf tw}\cdot k+{\sf tw}\log{\sf tw})}\cdot n
.

According to
Proposition 4.6
,
{\sf td}(G)\leq{\sf tw}(G)\cdot\log n
for any graph
G
.
Since
{\sf ed}_{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}(G)\leq{\sf td}(G)\leq{\sf tw}(G)\cdot\log n
,
Theorem 7.1
implies the existence of an
XP
-algorithm
for
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
parameterized by treewidth.

Corollary 7.2
.

For every finite collection of graphs
\mathcal{F}
, there exists an algorithm
that,
given a graph
G
of treewidth at most
{\sf tw}
, computes
{\sf ed}_{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}(G)
in time
n^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}({\sf tw}^{2})}
.

According to
Proposition 4.6
again,
{\sf tw}(G)\leq{\sf td}(G)
for any graph
G
.
Since we moreover have
{\sf ed}_{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}(G)\leq{\sf td}(G)
,
Theorem 7.1
implies the existence of an
FPT
-algorithm
for
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
parameterized by treedepth.

Corollary 7.3
.

For every finite collection of graphs
\mathcal{F}
, there exists an algorithm
that,
given a graph
G
of treedepth at most
{\sf td}
, computes
{\sf ed}_{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}(G)
in time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}({\sf td}^{2})}\cdot n
.

Our algorithm takes inspiration from the dynamic programming algorithm of Reidl, Rossmanith, Villaamil, and Sikdar
[
61
]
for treedepth.

Proposition 7.4

(
[
61
]
)
.

Given a graph
G
, a tree decomposition of
G
of width
w
, and an integer
k
, there is an algorithm that decides whether
{\sf td}(G)\leq k
in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k\cdot w)}\cdot n
.

If
\mathcal{F}=\{K_{1}\}
, elimination distance reduces to treedepth. Recall that
\{K_{1}\}
-M-Elimination Distance
is the problem asking whether
{\sf td}(G)\leq k
, which admits an algorithm in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k\cdot w)}\cdot n
because of
Proposition 7.4
.
Therefore, we may assume throughout this section that
\mathcal{F}
is non-trivial in order to have the useful property that a graph with a single vertex belongs to
\mathcal{G}={\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
. This will simplify the algorithm.
Moreover, the elimination distance to
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
of a disconnected graph is the maximum of the elimination distance of its connected components, and therefore, we may assume that the considered graphs and boundaried graphs are connected.

Compared to the approach of
[
61
]
, in order to deal with a general minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{G}
, we use the framework introduced in
[
9
]
based on the notion of representatives of an appropriately defined equivalence relation on boundaried graphs. Intuitively, since the “leaves” of the desired elimination tree are graphs in the minor-closed family
\mathcal{G}
, it will be possible to encode those graphs via their corresponding representatives. The fact that the boundary size that we need to consider is bounded follows from the description of the algorithm and its analysis.

In order to describe our dynamic programming algorithm, we have to describe its corresponding tables,
encode “partial elimination sets”, and show how to calculate this information using a nice tree decomposition of the input graph.
For this reason, in
Subsection 7.1
we start by giving some additional notations on functions on sets and in

Subsection 7.2
we define annotated trees. Annotated trees are labeled rooted trees that come together
with a boundaried graph such that the annotated nodes of the tree are mapped to the
vertices of the boundaried graph with the same label.
This notion is used in
Subsection 7.3
in order to define the
characteristic

of a boundaried graph, which intuitively encodes how partial elimination trees can be present inside the boundaried graph.
Forget, introduce, and join procedures that shall be used in the dynamic program on nice tree decompositions
are presented in
Subsection 7.4
.
In
Subsection 7.5
, we present the dynamic program and prove its correctness.
We conclude this section with
Subsection 7.6
, where we show that boundaried graphs
with the same characteristic can be exchanged, i.e., give graphs of the same elimination distance to
\mathcal{F}
when “glued” to the same boundaried graph. This latter result will also be used in
Section 10
.

7.1
Some additional notation

We denote by
{\sf Im}(f)
the image of a function
f
and by
{\sf Ker}(f)
its kernel, i.e., the elements whose image by
f
is 0.
Given two sets
A
and
B
, two subsets
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
and
B^{\prime}\subseteq B
, a function
f:A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
,
a\in A
, and
b\in B
,
f\oplus[a\mapsto b]
is the function that maps
a
to
b
and every
a^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}\setminus\{a\}
to
f(a^{\prime})
.
If
f:A\to B
, we denote by
f|_{A^{\prime}}
the restriction of
f
to
A^{\prime}
.
When
f
is a bijection,
f|_{A^{\prime}}:A^{\prime}\to{\sf Im}(A^{\prime})
is seen as a bijection.

(i\leftrightarrow j)
denotes the transposition of
i
and
j
, for
i,j
in some set
I\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}
.

7.2
Annotated trees

We proceed to define annotated trees, which we will use to codify the tables of our dynamic program.

Annotated trees.

An
annotated tree
is a tuple
\hat{T}=(T,r,h,{\bf R},f)
, where
(T,r)
is a rooted tree,
h:V(T)\to{\mathbb{N}}
,
{\bf R}=(R,B,\phi)
is a boundaried graph, and
f:[|B|]\to V(T)
.
See
Figure 4
for an illustration of an annotated tree.
We stress that different integers in
[|B|]
can be mapped, via
f
, to the same node of
T
.
The
trivial annotated tree
, denoted by
\hat{{\mathds{1}}}
, is
(T,r,h,{\mathds{1}},f)
where
T
is the rooted tree with a single node
r
,

h
is the constant function 0,
{\mathds{1}}
is the boundaried graph with one single vertex that is also part of the boundary, and
f
maps 1 to
r
.
The
height
of an annotated tree is
h(r)
.
Given an annotated tree
\hat{T}=(T,r,h,(R,B,\phi),f)
, we refer to
(T,r)
as its
rooted tree
.
Given an annotated tree
\hat{T}=(T,r,h,(R,B,\phi),f)
and a permutation
\sigma
of
[|B|]
, we use
\sigma(\hat{T})
to denote
(T,r,h,(R,B,\sigma\circ\phi),f\circ\sigma^{-1})
.

Figure 4:
An annotated tree made of a rooted tree (left) and a boundaried graph (right).
The numbers in the left figure correspond to the pre-images of
f
for the nodes of
V(T)
and the numbers on the right figure correspond to the images of
\phi
.
The function
h
that gives a value to each node of the tree is not represented.

We also define the following operations on annotated trees, which will be used to combine the tables of the dynamic programming algorithm. The first one is inspired by a similar operation introduced in
[
61
]
.

Crop operation.

Given an annotated tree
\hat{T}=(T,r,h,(R,B,\phi),f)
, the
crop operation
, denoted by
{\sf crop}(\hat{T})
, outputs the annotated tree obtained from
\hat{T}
by iteratively removing the leaves of
T
that are not in
{\sf Im}(f)
.
Given a set
\mathcal{A}
of annotated trees,
{\sf crop}(\mathcal{A}):=\bigcup_{\hat{T}\in\mathcal{A}}{\sf crop}(\hat{T})
.

Representation operation.

Given an annotated tree
\hat{T}=(T,r,h,(R,B,\phi),f)
, the
representation operation
, denoted by
{\sf rep}(\hat{T})
, outputs the annotated tree
\hat{T}^{\prime}=(T,r,h,(R^{\prime},B,\phi),f)
constructed as follows.
For each
v\in{\sf Im}(f)
, let
B_{v}:=\phi^{-1}\circ f^{-1}(v)
, let
\sigma_{v}:f^{-1}(v)\to[|B_{v}|]
be a bijective function, and let
R_{v}
be the union of the connected components of
R
containing
B_{v}
.
If there is a node
v\in{\sf Im}(f)
such that
R_{v}\notin{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
, then
R^{\prime}:=K_{s_{\mathcal{F}}}
.
Otherwise, for each
v\in{\sf Im}(f)
, let
(R_{v}^{\prime},B_{v},\sigma_{v}\circ\phi|_{B_{v}})\in\mathcal{R}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}^{|B_{v}|}
be the representative of
(R_{v},B_{v},\sigma_{v}\circ\phi|_{B_{v}})
for the equivalence relation
\equiv_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}
.
Then
R^{\prime}=\bigcup_{v\in{\sf Im}(f)}R_{v}^{\prime}
.
Intuitively, if there is a node
v\in{\sf Im}(f)
such that
R_{v}\notin{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
, to store this information it suffices to set
R^{\prime}:=K_{s_{\mathcal{F}}}
, while otherwise, we keep for each
R_{v}
(in fact, for the boundaried version of
R_{v}
) its representative.

An example of the crop and representation operation is give in
Figure 5
.
Observe that
{\sf rep}(\hat{{\mathds{1}}})=\hat{{\mathds{1}}}
since this is a minimum-sized representative and since we make the assumption that
\mathcal{F}
is non-trivial.
Given a set
\mathcal{A}
of annotated trees,
{\sf rep}(\mathcal{A}):=\bigcup_{\hat{T}\in\mathcal{A}}{\sf rep}(\hat{T})
.

Figure 5:
The crop and representation operation applied to the annotated tree of
Figure 4
. The unlabeled leaves of the tree are iteratively removed. A representative of each component attached to the boundary of the boundaried graph is kept.

Filter operation.

Given a set
\mathcal{A}
of annotated trees and a positive integer
k
, the
filter operation
, denoted by
{\sf filter}_{k}
,
outputs the set of annotated trees in
\mathcal{A}
with height at most
k
.

Note that the crop, representation, and filter operations are commutative since they do not modify the same objects.
For more simplicity, we define
\mathcal{M}_{k}={\sf filter}_{k}\circ{\sf rep}\circ{\sf crop}
.
We stress that
\mathcal{M}_{k}
is an operation acting on
sets
of annotated trees.

7.3
Characteristic of a boundaried graph

In this subsection we define the
characteristic
of a boundaried graph that shall be computed by the dynamic program in
Subsection 7.5
. This characteristic will consist of a set of annotated trees with some additional properties.
In order to present this definition, we first define the complete characteristic of a boundaried graph, that is a slightly more complicated way to see
\mathcal{F}
-elimination trees with some distinguished nodes.

Complete characteristic of a boundaried graph.

Given a connected boundaried graph
{\bf G}=(G,X,\rho)
, the
complete characteristic
of
{\bf G}
, denoted by
{\sf char^{*}}({\bf G})
, is the set of annotated trees
\hat{T}=(T,r,h,{\bf R},f)
such that

•

|{\sf Im}(f)|=|X|
,

•

there exists a function
\chi:V(T)\to 2^{V(G)}
such that
(T,\chi,r)
is an
\mathcal{F}
-elimination tree of
G
and for
x\in X
,
x\in\chi\circ f\circ\rho(x)
,

•

there exists an isomorphism
\sigma
between
{\bf R}
and
(\bigcup_{v\in{\sf Im}(f)}G[\chi(v)],X,\rho)
, and

•

h
is the height function
0pt_{T,r}
.

(\chi,\sigma)
is called the
witness pair
of
\hat{T}
with respect to
{\bf G}
.
Since
(T,\chi,r)
is an
\mathcal{F}
-elimination tree, it is straightforward to see that for any boundaried graph
{\bf G}
with underlying graph
G
, the minimum height of an annotated tree in
{\sf char^{*}}({\bf G})
is
{\sf ed}_{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}(G)
.

Characteristic of a boundaried graph.

Let
{\bf G}=(G,X,\rho)
be a boundaried graph and
k
be an integer. The
characteristic
of
{\bf G}
, denoted by
{\sf char}_{k}({\bf G})
, is the set
\mathcal{M}_{k}({\sf char^{*}}({\bf G}))
.

Lemma 7.5
.

Given a boundaried graph
{\bf G}=(G,X,\rho)
with
X\neq\emptyset
and an integer
k
, the elimination distance of
G
to
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
is the minimum height of an annotated tree in
{\sf char}_{k}({\bf G})
if
{\sf ed}_{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}(G)\leq k
, and
{\sf char}_{k}({\bf G})=\emptyset
otherwise.

Proof 7.6
.

Let
{\bf G}=(G,X,\rho)
be a boundaried graph with
X\neq\emptyset
and let
\hat{T}\in{\sf char^{*}}({\bf G})
.
Since
X\neq\emptyset
, the crop operation will not remove the root of the underlying tree of
\hat{T}
.
Moreover, neither the crop operation nor the representation operation change the height of the nodes that stay in the tree.
So the height of
{\sf rep}\circ{\sf crop}(\hat{T})
is equal to the height of
\hat{T}
.

We now prove that the size of the characteristic of a boundaried graph is upper-bounded by a function of its boundary size and
k
.

Lemma 7.7
.

There exists a function
f_{14}:{\mathbb{N}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{N}}
such that,
given two integers
k
and
w
, if
{\bf G}=(G,X,\rho)
is a boundaried graph with
|X|\leq w
, then
|{\sf char}_{k}({\bf G})|\leq\hyperref@@ii[@caricatures]{f_{\ref*{@caricatures}}}(w,k)
.
Moreover,
\hyperref@@ii[@caricatures]{f_{\ref*{@caricatures}}}(w,k)=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(w\cdot k+w\log w)}
.

Proof 7.8
.

Let
\hat{T}=(T,r,h,{\bf R},f)
be an annotated tree in
{\sf char}_{k}({\bf G})
. Let
l:=|X|
.
Let
x_{1},...,x_{l}
be an ordering of the nodes in
{\sf Im}(f)
such that if
x_{i}\in{\sf Anc}_{T,r}(x_{j})
, then
i<j
.
Without loss of generality, we suppose that
f(i)=x_{i}
for
i\in[l]
(this is true up to a permutation of
[l]
).
Let
f_{i}
be the restriction of
f
to
[i]
, let
T_{i}
be the tree obtained from
T
by iteratively removing the leaves not in
{\sf Im}(f_{i})
, and let
h_{i}
be the restriction of
h
to
V(T_{i})
.
Note that
{\sf Im}(h_{i})\subseteq[0,k]
and
T_{i}
is a tree with at most
i
leaves because the leaves of
T
are in
{\sf Im}(f_{i})
and
|{\sf Im}(f_{i})|=i
.
So
T_{i}
has at most
i\cdot(k+1)
nodes.

We set
(T_{0},h_{0},f_{0})
to be the empty triple.
Let us bound the number of triples
(T_{i},h_{i},f_{i})
that can be constructed from
(T_{i-1},h_{i-1},f_{i-1})
for
i\in[l]
.
For
i\in[l]
, we can construct
(T_{i},h_{i},f_{i})
from
(T_{i-1},h_{i-1},f_{i-1})
by choosing a node of
T_{i-1}
(if it exists) and adding a path of length at most
k
with leaf
x_{i}
(all nodes in this path are new, except from
x_{i}
).
We consider the function
h_{i}
that has the same values as
h_{i-1}
on
V(T_{i-1})
and values in
[0,k]
on the new path such that the value of
h_{i}
strictly increases from a leaf to the root
r
.
Observe that the value of
h_{i}
on the new path is a subset of
2^{[k+1]}
.
Therefore,
the number of different triples
(T,h,f)
is at most

\prod_{i=1}^{w}i(k+1)2^{k+1}=w!(k+1)^{w}2^{w(k+1)}\leq 2^{w\log w+w\log(k+1)+w(k+1)}.

Since
\hat{T}=(T,r,h,{\bf R},f)
is an annotated tree in
{\sf char}_{k}({\bf G})
, it holds that
{\bf R}
is the union of
l
representatives
{\bf R}_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}^{w_{i}}
for
i\in[l]
where
l=|{\sf Im}(f)|
, such that
\sum_{i=1}^{l}w_{i}=l\leq w
.
By
Proposition 4.12
,
|\mathcal{R}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}^{w_{i}}|=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(w_{i}\log w_{i})}
.
So the number of ways to construct
{\bf R}
is bounded by

\prod_{i=1}^{l}2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(w_{i}\log w_{i})}=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(\sum_{i=1}^{l}w_{i}\log w)}=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(w\log w)}.

Hence, we obtain the desired result.

7.4
The procedures

We define here the procedures that will be used in the dynamic programming algorithm.
Given a nice tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}
of a graph
G
, we want to define forget, introduce, and join procedures to obtain the characteristic of
G_{v}
for each internal node
v
in
\mathcal{T}
, given the characteristics of
G_{v^{\prime}}
for each child
v^{\prime}
of
v
.
Before defining the procedures for the characteristics, we define the procedures for the complete characteristics.

7.4.1
Forget procedure

With the forget procedure, given the characteristic of a boundaried graph, we want to compute the characteristic of the boundaried graph obtained by removing a vertex from the boundary.

Complete forget procedure.

The complete forget procedure applied on the annotated tree
(T,r,h,{\bf R},f)
corresponds to removing the vertex with the largest label from the boundary of
{\bf R}
.
More formally, given an annotated tree
\hat{T}=(T,r,h,(R,B,\phi),f)
, the
complete forget procedure
, denoted by
{\sf forget}^{*}(\hat{T})
, outputs the annotated tree
\hat{T}^{\prime}=(T,r,h,(R,B^{\prime},\phi|_{B^{\prime}}),f|_{[|B^{\prime}|]}))
, where
B^{\prime}=B\setminus\phi^{-1}(|B|)
.
Given a set
\mathcal{A}
of annotated trees,
{\sf forget}^{*}(\mathcal{A}):=\bigcup_{\hat{T}\in\mathcal{A}}{\sf forget}^{*}(\hat{T})
.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
G
be a graph,
({\sf T},\beta,{\sf r})

be a nice tree decomposition of
G
,
v
be a forget node of
{\sf T}
with child
v^{\prime}
and forgotten vertex
x
,
\rho:\beta(v)\to[|\beta(v)|]
be a bijection, and
\rho^{\prime}:=\rho\oplus[x\mapsto|\beta(v^{\prime})|]
.
Let
\mathcal{A}:={\sf char^{*}}(G_{v},\beta(v),\rho)
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}:={\sf char^{*}}(G_{v^{\prime}},\beta(v^{\prime}),\rho^{\prime})
.
Then
\mathcal{A}={\sf forget}^{*}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
.

Proof 7.10
.

Let
\hat{T}\in{\sf forget}^{*}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
. There exists
\hat{T}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
with witness pair
(\chi^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})

such that
{\sf forget}^{*}(\hat{T}^{\prime})=\hat{T}
.
Let
(T,r)
(resp.
(T^{\prime},r)
) be the rooted tree of
\hat{T}
(resp.
\hat{T}^{\prime}
).
Note that
0pt_{T,r}=0pt_{T^{\prime},r}
.
Thus, it is easy to see that
(\chi^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})
also witnesses that
\hat{T}\in\mathcal{A}
.
Conversely, let
\hat{T}=(T,r,h,(R,X,\phi),f)\in\mathcal{A}
with witness pair
(\chi,\sigma)
. Let
u:=\chi^{-1}(x)
,
w:=\sigma^{-1}(x)
, and
t=|X|+1
.
Let
\hat{T}^{\prime}:=(T,r,h,(R,X\cup\{w\},\phi\oplus[w\mapsto t]),f\oplus[t\mapsto u])
.
Then
{\sf forget}^{*}(\hat{T}^{\prime})=\hat{T}
and the pair
(\chi,\sigma)
also witnesses that
\hat{T}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, so
\hat{T}\in{\sf forget}^{*}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
.

Forget procedure.

Given an annotated tree
\hat{T}
, the
forget procedure
, denoted by
{\sf forget}(\hat{T})
, outputs
{\sf rep}\circ{\sf crop}\circ{\sf forget}^{*}(\hat{T})
.
See
Figure 6
for an illustration.
Note that we do not apply the filter operation since the height does not change under the forget procedure.
Given a set
\mathcal{A}
of annotated trees,
{\sf forget}(\mathcal{A})
outputs
\bigcup_{\hat{T}\in\mathcal{A}}{\sf forget}(\hat{T})
.

Figure 6:
The forget procedure applied to the annotated tree of
Figure 4
.

Lemma 7.11
.

Let
G
be a graph,
k
be an integer,
({\sf T},\beta,{\sf r})
be a nice tree decomposition of
G
,
v
be a forget node of
{\sf T}
with child
v^{\prime}
and forgotten vertex
x
,
\rho:\beta(v)\to[|\beta(v)|]
be a bijection, and
\rho^{\prime}:=\rho\oplus[x\mapsto|\beta(v^{\prime})|]
.
Let
\mathcal{A}:={\sf char}_{k}(G_{v},\beta(v),\rho)
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}:={\sf char}_{k}(G_{v^{\prime}},\beta(v^{\prime}),\rho^{\prime})
.
Then
\mathcal{A}={\sf forget}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
.

Proof 7.12
.

Let
\mathcal{D}:={\sf char^{*}}(G_{v},\beta(v),\rho)
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}:={\sf char^{*}}(G_{v^{\prime}},\beta(v^{\prime}),\rho^{\prime})
.
According to
Lemma 7.9
,
\mathcal{D}={\sf forget}^{*}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
.
The representation and the crop operation do not change the labels of an annotated tree, while the complete forget procedure only changes the labels of the input annotated tree and it does not change its height.
Thus,
{\sf rep}\circ{\sf forget}^{*}={\sf rep}\circ{\sf forget}^{*}\circ{\sf rep}
,
{\sf crop}\circ{\sf forget}^{*}={\sf crop}\circ{\sf forget}^{*}\circ{\sf crop}
, and
{\sf filter}_{k}\circ{\sf forget}^{*}={\sf forget}^{*}\circ{\sf filter}_{k}
.
Since the three operations are commutative, it follows that
\mathcal{M}_{k}\circ{\sf forget}^{*}={\sf rep}\circ{\sf crop}\circ{\sf forget}^{*}\circ\mathcal{M}_{k}={\sf forget}\circ\mathcal{M}_{k}
.
Hence,
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{M}_{k}(\mathcal{D})=\mathcal{M}_{k}\circ{\sf forget}^{*}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})={\sf forget}(\mathcal{M}_{k}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}))={\sf forget}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
.

7.4.2
Introduce procedure

With the introduce procedure, given the characteristic of a boundaried graph and a set
I
of labels from the boundary, we want to compute the characteristic of the boundaried graph obtained by adding a new vertex to the boundary, which is adjacent to the nodes with a label in
I
.

Diamond-introduce operation.

Let
(T,r)
be a rooted tree,
w
be an integer,
f:[w]\to V(T)
be a function, and
I
be a subset of
[w]
.

(T,r,f)\diamondsuit_{\sf intr}I
is defined as the set of all pairs
(T^{\prime},r^{\prime},f^{\prime})
such that:

1.

(T^{\prime},r^{\prime})
is a rooted tree,

2.

V(T^{\prime})=V(T)\cup\{u\}
for some new node
u
,

3.

f^{\prime}=f\oplus[w+1\mapsto u]
,

4.

if
v_{1}\in V(T)
and
v_{2}\in{\sf Anc}_{T,r}(v_{1})
, then
v_{2}\in{\sf Anc}_{T^{\prime},r^{\prime}}(v_{1})
,

5.

if
v\in f(I)
, then
v\in{\sf Anc}_{T,r}(u)\cup{\sf Desc}_{T,r}(u)
, and

6.

T_{u}^{\prime}\cap f(I)\neq\emptyset
, or
u\in{\sf Leaf}(T^{\prime},r^{\prime})
and
{\sf Par}_{T^{\prime},r^{\prime}}(u)\in f(I)
.

This operation corresponds to introducing a new node
u
in
T
so that
u
has ancestor-descendant relations with the nodes labeled by a label in
I
.
The last item, which states that
u
either has a descendant in
f(I)
or is a leaf and its parent belongs to
f(I)
, is a property needed to ensure connectivity and allows the application of the crop operation in
Lemma 7.13
and
Lemma 7.15
.

Let
(T,r)
be a rooted tree,
K
be a subset of
V(T)
, and
h:K\to{\mathbb{N}}
.
We define the function
{\sf update}_{T,r,K}(h):V(T)\to{\mathbb{N}}
,
that maps every
v\in V(T)
to the integer

{\sf update}_{T,r,K}(h)(v)=\max\{h(v),1+\max_{c\in{\sf Ch}(v)}\{{\sf update}_{T,r,K}(h)(c)\}\},

where we suppose that
h(v)=0
if
v\notin K
and
{\sf update}_{T,r,K}(h)(c)=-1
if
v
is childless.
Let
(T^{\prime},r^{\prime})
be a rooted tree with
V(T^{\prime})=K
and such that the ancestor-descendant relationship between the nodes of
K
is the same in
(T,r)
and
(T^{\prime},r^{\prime})
.
Then we can observe that
{\sf update}_{T,r,K}(0pt_{T^{\prime},r^{\prime}})=0pt_{T,r}
.

Complete introduce procedure.

Let
\hat{T}=(T,r,h,(R,X,\phi),f)
be an annotated tree and let
I
be a set of labels in
[|X|]
.
The complete introduce procedure corresponds to adding a new vertex
v
to the boundary
X
of the boundaried graph
(R,X,\phi)
of an annotated tree
(T,r,h,(R,X,\phi),f)
, such that
v
is adjacent to the nodes with a label in
I
and it is either mapped, via
f\circ\phi
, to an already existing leaf of
T
(item (a) below) or to a new node of
T
(item (b) below).

More formally, given an annotated tree
\hat{T}=(T,r,h,(R,X,\phi),f)
, a set
I\subseteq[|X|]
of labels, the
complete introduce procedure
, denoted by
{\sf intr}^{*}(\hat{T},I)
, outputs a set
\mathcal{A}
of annotated trees constructed as follows.
For each
(T^{\prime},r^{\prime},f^{\prime})\in(T,r,f)\diamondsuit_{\sf intr}I
, let
w=|{\sf Im}(f^{\prime})|
,
u:=f^{\prime}(w)
, and
u^{\prime}:={\sf Par}_{T^{\prime},r^{\prime}}(u)
(or
u^{\prime}:=u
if
u
is the root).
We add a new vertex
v
to
R
and we set
X^{\prime}:=X\cup\{v\}
and
\phi^{\prime}:=\phi\oplus[v\mapsto w]
.
Let
(R_{u^{\prime}},X_{u^{\prime}},\phi_{u^{\prime}})
be the part of the representative
R
corresponding to
u^{\prime}
, as defined for the representation operation (i.e.,
R_{u^{\prime}}
is the union of the connected components of
R
containing
\phi^{-1}\circ f^{-1}(u^{\prime})
,
X_{u^{\prime}}=\phi^{-1}\circ f^{-1}(u^{\prime})
,
\phi_{u^{\prime}}=\sigma_{u^{\prime}}\circ\phi|_{X_{u^{\prime}}}
and
\sigma_{u^{\prime}}:f^{-1}(u^{\prime})\to[|X_{u^{\prime}}|]
is a bijective function).

(a)

If
h(u^{\prime})=0
:

We set
f^{\prime\prime}:=f\oplus[w\mapsto u^{\prime}]

and
R^{\prime}:=(V(R)\cup\{v\},E(R)\cup E(\{v\},\phi^{-1}(I)\cap X_{u^{\prime}}))

and we add
(T,r,h,(R^{\prime},X^{\prime},\phi^{\prime}),f^{\prime\prime})
to
\mathcal{A}

if the connected component of
R^{\prime}
containing
v
belongs to
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
.

(b)

If
u^{\prime}\notin{\sf Im}(f)
, or if
u^{\prime}\in{\sf Im}(f)
and
|V(R_{u^{\prime}})|=1
:

We set
R^{\prime}:=(V(R)\cup\{v\},E(R))

and
h^{\prime}:={\sf update}_{T^{\prime},r^{\prime},V(T)}(h)
and
we add
(T^{\prime},r^{\prime},h^{\prime},(R^{\prime},X,^{\prime}\phi^{\prime}),f^{\prime})
to
\mathcal{A}
.

Note that this is not a dichotomy: if both criteria are fulfilled, then we apply both cases.
Given a set
\mathcal{A}
of annotated trees, we define
{\sf intr}^{*}(\mathcal{A},I):=\bigcup_{\hat{T}\in\mathcal{A}}{\sf intr}^{*}(\hat{T},I)
.

Lemma 7.13
.

Let
G
be a graph,
({\sf T},\beta,{\sf r})
be a nice tree decomposition of
G
,
v
be an introduce node of
{\sf T}
with child
v^{\prime}
and introduced vertex
x
,
\rho^{\prime}:\beta(v)\to[|\beta(v)|]
be a bijection,
\rho:=\rho^{\prime}\oplus[x\mapsto|\beta(v)|]
, and
I:=\rho(N_{G_{v}}(x))
.
Let
\mathcal{A}:={\sf char^{*}}(G_{v},\beta(v),\rho)
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}:={\sf char^{*}}(G_{v^{\prime}},\beta(v^{\prime}),\rho^{\prime})
.
Then
\mathcal{A}={\sf intr}^{*}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},I)
.

Proof 7.14
.

Let
\hat{T}=(T,r,h,(R,X,\phi),f)\in{\sf intr}^{*}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},I)
.
There is
\hat{T}^{\prime}=(T^{\prime},r^{\prime},h^{\prime},{\bf R}^{\prime},f^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
with witness pair
(\chi^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})
such that
\hat{T}\in{\sf intr}^{*}(\hat{T^{\prime}},I)
.
Let
\chi:=\chi^{\prime}\oplus[u\mapsto x]
and
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}\oplus[w\to x]
where
u:=f(|X|)
and
w:=\phi^{-1}(|X|)
.

Suppose that we are in case (a).
Thus,
h=h^{\prime}=0pt_{T^{\prime},r^{\prime}}=0pt_{T,r}
.
By the construction of
{\bf R}
from
{\bf R}^{\prime}
, since
x
is only adjacent in
G_{v}
to the nodes with a label in
I
,
\sigma
is an isomorphism between
{\bf R}
and
(\bigcup_{w\in{\sf Im}(f)}G_{v}[\chi(w)],\beta(v),\rho)
.
Moreover,
T=T^{\prime}
so
(T,\chi,r)
is an
\mathcal{F}
-elimination tree of
G_{v}
.
Hence,
(\chi,\sigma)
witnesses that
\hat{T}\in\mathcal{A}
.

Suppose now that we are in case (b).
Notice that in this case

h={\sf update}_{T,r,V(T^{\prime})}(h^{\prime})={\sf update}_{T,r,V(T^{\prime})}(0pt_{T^{\prime},r^{\prime}})=0pt_{T,r}.

It is easy to see that
\sigma
is an isomorphism between
{\bf R}
and
(\bigcup_{w\in{\sf Im}(f)}G_{v}[\chi(w)],\beta(v),\rho)
.
Moreover, as
\hat{T}\in{\sf intr}^{*}(\hat{T^{\prime}},I)
, it holds that
(T,r,f)\in(T^{\prime},r^{\prime},f^{\prime})\diamondsuit_{\sf intr}I
and therefore
(T,r)
keeps the ancestor-descendant relations from
(T^{\prime},r^{\prime})
(item 4 of the
\diamondsuit_{\sf intr}
operation), while adding ancestor-descendant relations between the new node of
T
and the nodes labeled by
I
(item 5),
and guaranteeing the connectivity of
G_{v}[\chi(T_{w})]
for each node
w\in V(T)
(item 6).
Hence,
(\chi,\sigma)
witnesses that
\hat{T}\in\mathcal{A}
.

Conversely, let
\hat{T}=(T,r,h,{\bf R},f)\in\mathcal{A}
with witness pair
(\chi,\sigma)
.
If
\chi(\chi^{-1}(x))=\{x\}
, then let
T^{\prime}
be the tree obtained from
T
by removing
u:=\chi^{-1}(x)
and adding edges between the parent of
u
and the children of
u
, if any. Otherwise, set
T^{\prime}:=T
.
Let
h^{\prime}
be the height function of
T^{\prime}
and
r^{\prime}
be the root of
T^{\prime}
.
Let
(R^{\prime},X^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})
be the boundaried graph obtained from
{\bf R}
by removing
\sigma^{-1}(x)
.
Let
f^{\prime}:=f|_{X^{\prime}}
.
Then the functions obtained from
\chi
and
\sigma
after restricting their image to
V(G)\setminus x
witness that
\hat{T}^{\prime}=(T^{\prime},r^{\prime},h^{\prime},(R^{\prime},X^{\prime},\phi^{\prime}),f^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.
To show that
\hat{T}\in{\sf intr}^{*}(\hat{T^{\prime}},I)
, the only non-trivial part is to prove that either
T_{u}\cap f(I)\neq\emptyset
, or
u\in{\sf Leaf}(T,r)
and
{\sf Par}_{T,r}(u)\in f(I)
(item 6 of the
\diamondsuit_{\sf intr}
operation).
Suppose that
T_{u}\cap f(I)=\emptyset
.
We know that
x
is exactly adjacent to
\rho^{-1}(I)
in
G_{v}
since it is an introduce vertex.
Moreover,
(T,\chi,r)
is an
\mathcal{F}
-elimination tree of
G_{v}
, so
G_{v}[\chi(T_{w})]
is connected for all
w\in V(T)
.
In particular,
G_{v}[\chi(T_{u})]
is connected, but
x\in G_{v}[\chi(T_{u})]
and
\rho^{-1}(I)\notin G_{v}[\chi(T_{u})]
because
T_{u}\cap f(I)=\emptyset
. Thus, we must have
G_{v}[\chi(T_{u})]=\{x\}
, and so
u\in{\sf Leaf}(T,r)
.
Since
G_{v}[\chi(T_{{\sf Par}_{T,r}(u)})]
is also connected, it implies that
\chi({\sf Par}_{T,r}(u))
and
x
are connected, so
{\sf Par}_{T,r}(u)\in f(I)
.
Hence,
\hat{T}\in{\sf intr}^{*}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},I)
.

Introduce procedure.

Given an annotated tree
\hat{T}
, a set
I
of labels, and a positive integer
k
, the
introduce procedure
, denoted by
{\sf intr}_{k}(\hat{T},I)
, outputs
{\sf filter}_{k}\circ{\sf rep}\circ{\sf intr}^{*}(\hat{T},I)
.
Examples of the introduce procedure can be found in
Figure 7
.
Note that we do not apply the crop operation, since the complete introduction procedure applied to a cropped annotated tree outputs a cropped annotated tree.
Given a set
\mathcal{A}
of annotated trees,
{\sf intr}_{k}(\mathcal{A},I)
outputs
\bigcup_{\hat{T}\in\mathcal{A}}{\sf intr}_{k}(\hat{T},I)
.

Figure 7:
Two annotated trees obtained from the introduce procedure applied to the annotated tree of
Figure 4
with
I=\{2,6\}
.

Lemma 7.15
.

Let
G
be a graph,
({\sf T},\beta,{\sf r})
be a nice tree decomposition of
G
,
v
be an introduce node of
{\sf T}
with child
v^{\prime}
and introduced vertex
x
,
\rho^{\prime}:\beta(v)\to[|\beta(v)|]
be a bijection,
\rho:=\rho^{\prime}\oplus[x\mapsto|\beta(v)|]
, and
I:=\rho(N_{G_{v}}(x))
.
Let
k
be an integer,
\mathcal{A}:={\sf char}_{k}(G_{v},\beta(v),\rho)
, and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}:={\sf char}_{k}(G_{v^{\prime}},\beta(v^{\prime}),\rho^{\prime})
.
Then
