\mathcal{A}={\sf intr}_{k}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},I)
.

Proof 7.16
.

Let
\mathcal{D}:={\sf char^{*}}(G_{v},\beta(v),\rho)
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}:={\sf char^{*}}(G_{v^{\prime}},\beta(v^{\prime}),\rho^{\prime})
.
According to
Lemma 7.13
,
\mathcal{D}={\sf intr}^{*}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime},I)
.

The crop operation acts on the tree
T
of an annotated tree
(T,r,h,{\bf R},f)
to remove the leaves that are not in
{\sf Im}(f)
.
By item 6 of the
\diamondsuit_{\sf intr}
operation, the complete introduce procedure may only add a new node in
T
above a node in
{\sf Im}(f)
, or add a leaf to
T
with a parent node in
{\sf Im}(f)
.
Hence, the new node may only be added to the cropped tree.
Since the new vertex is also in the boundary, it implies that, given a set
\mathcal{C}
of annotated trees,
{\sf crop}\circ{\sf intr}^{*}(\mathcal{C},I)={\sf intr}^{*}({\sf crop}(\mathcal{C}),I)
.

The representation operation acts on
{\bf R}
to replace it by a boundaried graph whose connected components are representatives of the connected components of
{\bf R}
.
The complete introduce procedure adds a new vertex in the boundary of
{\bf R}
that is adjacent to boundary vertices only.
Hence,
{\sf rep}\circ{\sf intr}^{*}(\mathcal{C},I)={\sf rep}\circ{\sf intr}^{*}({\sf rep}(\mathcal{C}),I)
.

Moreover, the complete introduce procedure can only increase the height of an annotated tree.
Therefore,
{\sf filter}_{k}\circ{\sf intr}^{*}(\mathcal{C},I)={\sf filter}_{k}\circ{\sf intr}^{*}({\sf filter}_{k}(\mathcal{C}),I)
.

Since the three operations are commutative, we have that
\mathcal{M}_{k}\circ{\sf intr}^{*}(\mathcal{C},I)={\sf filter}_{k}\circ{\sf rep}\circ{\sf intr}^{*}(\mathcal{M}_{k}(\mathcal{C}),I)={\sf intr}_{k}(\mathcal{M}_{k}(\mathcal{C}),I)
.
Therefore,
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{M}_{k}(\mathcal{D})=\mathcal{M}_{k}\circ{\sf intr}^{*}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime},I)={\sf intr}_{k}(\mathcal{M}_{k}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}),I)={\sf intr}_{k}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},I)
.

For the introduce procedure, we finally prove that it can generate a bounded number of annotated trees.

Lemma 7.17
.

Let
w
and
k
be two positive integers, let
{\bf G}
be a
w
-boundaried graph, let
I\subseteq[w]
, and let
\hat{T}\in{\sf char}_{k}({\bf G})
.
Then
|{\sf intr}_{k}(\hat{T},I)|=\mathcal{O}(w\cdot k)
.

Proof 7.18
.

Let
\hat{T}=(T,r,h,{\bf R},f)
.
Let us show that
|(T,r,f)\diamondsuit_{\sf intr}I|=\mathcal{O}(w\cdot k)
.
Since
{\sf Leaf}(T,r)\subseteq{\sf Im}(f)
,
T
has at most
w
leaves.
Thus,
T
has at most
w\cdot(k+1)
nodes.
The
\diamondsuit_{\sf intr}
operation consists in adding a new node to
T
, either as the new parent of a node, or as a new leaf of
T
.
Therefore,
|(T,r,f)\diamondsuit_{\sf intr}I|\leq 2w\cdot(k+1)
.

Let
(T^{\prime},r^{\prime},f^{\prime})\in(T,r,f)\diamondsuit_{\sf intr}I
.
In the introduction procedure, we obtain at most two annotated trees from
(T^{\prime},r^{\prime},f^{\prime})
.
Therefore,
|{\sf intr}_{k}(\hat{T},I)|\leq 4w\cdot(k+1)
.

7.4.3
Join procedure

With the join procedure, given the characteristic of two boundaried graphs
{\bf G}_{1}
and
{\bf G}_{2}
that are compatible, we want to compute the characteristic of
{\bf G}_{1}\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.4}{$\oplus$}}}{\bf G}_{2}
.

Diamond-join operation.

Let
(T_{1},r_{1})
and
(T_{2},r_{2})
be two rooted trees,
w
be an integer, and
f_{1}:[w]\to V(T_{1})
and
f_{2}:[w]\to V(T_{2})
be two functions such that
\{f_{1}^{-1}(u)\mid u\in{\sf Im}(f_{1})\}=\{f_{2}^{-1}(u)\mid u\in{\sf Im}(f_{2})\}
.
Thanks to this equality, we can identify
f_{1}
with
f_{2}
and say that
V(T_{1})\cap V(T_{2})={\sf Im}(f_{1})
.
We define
(T_{1},r_{1},f_{1})\diamondsuit_{\sf join}(T_{2},r_{2},f_{2})
as the set of all pairs
(T,r,f)
such that:

1.

f=f_{1}=f_{2}
,

2.

(T,r)
is a rooted tree,

3.

V(T)=V(T_{1})\cup V(T_{2})
,

4.

for
i\in\{1,2\}
, if
u\in V(T_{i})
and
v\in{\sf Anc}_{T_{i},r_{i}}(u)
, then
v\in{\sf Anc}_{T,r}(u)
, and

5.

for every
v\in{\sf Leaf}(T,r)
and every
w\in{\sf Anc}_{T,r}(v)
, if
V(vTw)\cap{\sf Im}(f)=\emptyset
, then there is an
i\in\{1,2\}
such that
vTw=vT_{i}w
.

The last item, which states that a branch of
T
that does not intersect
{\sf Im}(f)
either belongs to
T_{1}
or
T_{2}
, is a property needed to ensure connectivity and allows the application of the crop operation in
Lemma 7.19
and
Lemma 7.21
.

Let
(T,r)
be a rooted tree, and for
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
K_{i}\subseteq V(T)
and
h_{i}:K_{i}\to{\mathbb{N}}
.

{\sf update}_{T,r,K_{1},K_{2}}(h_{1},h_{2})
is the function that maps
v\in V(T)
to the maximum of

h_{1}(v)
,
h_{2}(v)
, and
\max_{c\in{\sf Ch}(v)}\{1+{\sf update}_{T,r,K_{1},K_{2}}(h_{1},h_{2})(c)\}
,

when they are defined.
For
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
(T_{i},r_{i})
be a rooted tree with
V(T_{i})=K_{i}
and such that the ancestor-descendant relationship between the nodes of
K_{i}
is the same in
T
and
T_{i}
.
Then we can observe that
{\sf update}_{T,r,K_{1},K_{2}}(0pt_{T_{1},r_{1}},0pt_{T_{2},r_{2}})=0pt_{T,r}
.

Complete join procedure.

The join procedure corresponds to “merging” two annotated trees whose intersection is exactly their boundary.
More formally, given two annotated trees
\hat{T}_{1}=(T_{1},r_{1},h_{1},{\bf R}_{1},f_{1})
and
\hat{T}_{2}=(T_{2},r_{2},h_{2},{\bf R}_{2},f_{2})
, the
complete join procedure
, denoted by
{\sf join}(\hat{T}_{1},\hat{T}_{2})
, outputs a set
\mathcal{A}
of annotated trees constructed as follows.
Initially,
\mathcal{A}
is empty.
If
{\bf R}_{1}
and
{\bf R}_{2}
are compatible (i.e., such that
\{f_{1}^{-1}(u)\mid u\in{\sf Im}(f_{1})\}=\{f_{2}^{-1}(u)\mid u\in{\sf Im}(f_{2})\}
) and such that
{\sf Ker}(h_{1}\circ f_{1})={\sf Ker}(h_{2}\circ f_{2})
, then for
(T,r,f)\in(T_{1},r_{1},f_{1})\diamondsuit_{\sf join}(T_{2},r_{2},f_{2})
, let
h:={\sf update}_{T,r,V(T_{1}),V(T_{2})}(h_{1},h_{2})
.
Let
(R,X,\rho):={\bf R}_{1}\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.4}{$\oplus$}}}{\bf R}_{2}
. If each connected component of
R
belongs to
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
, then we add
(T,r,h,{\bf R}_{1}\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.4}{$\oplus$}}}{\bf R}_{2},f)
to
\mathcal{A}
.
Given two sets
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
of annotated trees, we set
{\sf join}^{*}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}):=\bigcup_{\hat{T}_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{1},\hat{T}_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{2}}{\sf join}^{*}(\hat{T}_{1},\hat{T}_{2})
.

Lemma 7.19
.

Let
G
be a graph,
({\sf T},\beta,{\sf r})
be a nice tree decomposition of
G
,
v
be a join node of
{\sf T}
with children
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, and
\rho:\beta(v)\to[|\beta(v)|]
be a bijection.
Let
\mathcal{A}:={\sf char^{*}}(G_{v},\beta(v),\rho)
,
\mathcal{A}_{1}:={\sf char^{*}}(G_{v_{1}},\beta(v_{1}),\rho)
, and
\mathcal{A}_{2}:={\sf char^{*}}(G_{v_{2}},\beta(v_{2}),\rho)
.
Then
\mathcal{A}={\sf join}^{*}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
.

Proof 7.20
.

Let
\hat{T}=(T,r,h,(R,X,\phi),f)\in{\sf join}^{*}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
.
There is
\hat{T}_{1}=(T_{1},r_{1},h_{1},{\bf R}_{1},f_{1})\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\hat{T}_{2}=(T_{2},r_{2},h_{2},{\bf R}_{2},f_{2})\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
such that
\hat{T}\in{\sf join}^{*}(\hat{T}_{1},\hat{T}_{2})
with witness pair
(\chi_{1},\sigma_{1})
and
(\chi_{2},\sigma_{2})
, respectively, such that
\chi_{1|{\sf Im}(f)}=\chi_{2|{\sf Im}(f)}
and
\sigma_{1|X}=\sigma_{2|X}
.
Note that

h:={\sf update}_{T,r,V(T_{1}),V(T_{2})}(h_{1},h_{2})={\sf update}_{T,r,V(T_{1}),V(T_{2})}(0pt_{T_{1},r_{1}},0pt_{T_{2},r_{2}})=0pt_{T,r}.

Let
(\chi,\sigma)=(\chi_{1}\cup\chi_{2},\sigma_{1}\cup\sigma_{2})
.

It is easy to see that
\sigma
is an isomorphism between
{\bf R}
and
(\bigcup_{w\in{\sf Im}(f)}G[\chi(w)],\beta(v),\rho)
.
If
uv\in E(G_{v})
, since
G_{v_{1}}\setminus\beta(v)
and
G_{v_{2}}\setminus\beta(v)
are not connected, then there is
i\in\{1,2\}
such that
\chi_{i}(u)\in{\sf Anc}_{T,r}(\chi_{i}(v))\cup{\sf Desc}_{T,r}(\chi_{i}(v))
holds, so
\chi(u)\in{\sf Anc}_{T,r}(\chi(v))\cup{\sf Desc}_{T,r}(\chi(v))
due to item 4 of the
\diamondsuit_{\sf join}
operation.

Moreover, item 5 of the
\diamondsuit_{\sf join}
operation ensures the connectivity of
G_{v}[\chi(T_{w})]
for each
w\in V(T)
.
Indeed, let
i\in\{1,2\}
be such that
w\in V(T_{i})
. Suppose towards a contradiction that
G_{v}[\chi(T_{w})]
is not connected.
Note that it implies that
w\notin{\sf Im}(f)
, because
{\sf Im}(f)\subseteq V(T_{1})\cup V(T_{2})
and
(T_{1},\chi_{1},r_{1})
and
(T_{2},\chi_{2},r_{2})
are
\mathcal{F}
-elimination trees of
G_{v_{1}}
and
G_{v_{2}}
, respectively, so
G_{v_{1}}[\chi_{1}((T_{1})_{w})]
and
G_{v_{2}}[\chi_{2}((T_{2})_{w})]
are connected and therefore
G_{v}[\chi(T_{w})]
would be connected.
We assume that
w
is a minimal node such that
G_{v}[\chi(T_{w})]
is not connected, i.e., for every
u\in V(T_{w})\setminus\{w\}
,
G_{v}[\chi(T_{u})]
is connected.
Hence, there is
u\in{\sf Ch}_{T,r}(w)
such that
\chi(w)
is not connected to
G_{v}[\chi(T_{u})]
.
So
V(T_{u})\cup V(T_{i})=\emptyset
, since otherwise, the connectivity of
G_{v_{i}}[\chi_{i}((T_{i})_{w})]
would imply the connectivity of
\chi(w)
with
G_{v}[\chi(T_{u})]
.
Thus, there is an
x\in{\sf Leaf}(T,r)
such that
u\in{\sf Anc}_{T,r}(x)
, and therefore
w\in{\sf Anc}_{T,r}(x)
, and
V(xTw)\cap{\sf Im}(f)=\emptyset
.
So, according to item 5 of the
\diamondsuit_{\sf join}
operation, there is
i\in\{1,2\}
such that
xTw=xT_{i}w
.
This contradicts the fact that
w\in V(T_{i})\setminus{\sf Im}(f)
and
V(T_{u})\subseteq V(T_{j})\setminus{\sf Im}(f)
where
\{i,j\}=\{1,2\}
.
Thus,
(T,\chi,r)
is an
\mathcal{F}
-elimination tree of
G_{v}
.
Therefore,
(\chi,\sigma)
witnesses that
\hat{T}\in\mathcal{A}
.

Conversely, let
\hat{T}\in\mathcal{A}
with witness pair
(\chi,\sigma)
.
Let
(\chi_{1},\sigma_{1})
and
(\chi_{2},\sigma_{2})
be the co-restrictions of
(\chi,\sigma)
to
G_{v_{1}}
and
G_{v_{2}}
, respectively.

Let
T_{1}
be the tree obtained from
T
by removing the nodes not in
{\sf Im}(\chi_{1})
and adding edges between the parent and children of each removed node.
Let
r_{1}
be the root of
T_{1}
and
h_{1}:=0pt_{T_{1},r_{1}}
.
Let
{\bf R}_{1}
be obtained from
{\bf R}
by removing the vertices not in
{\sf Im}(\sigma_{1})
.
Then it is easy to see that
\hat{T}_{1}=(T_{1},r_{1},h_{1},{\bf R}_{1},f)
belongs to
B_{1}
with witness pair
(\chi_{1},\sigma_{1})
.
We construct similarly
\hat{T}_{2}=(T_{2},r_{2},h_{2},{\bf R}_{2},f)\in B_{2}
with witness pair
(\chi_{2},\sigma_{2})
.

Moreover, we claim that
\hat{T}\in{\sf join}^{*}(\hat{T}_{1},\hat{T}_{2})
.
To prove this claim, the less trivial part
is to show that
T
respects item 5 of the
\diamondsuit_{\sf join}
operation.
Let
x\in{\sf Leaf}(T,r)
and
w\in{\sf Anc}_{T,r}(x)
such that
V(xTw)\cap{\sf Im}(f)=\emptyset
.
Suppose towards a contradiction that
V(xTw)\cap V(T_{1})\neq\emptyset
and
V(xTw)\cap V(T_{2})\neq\emptyset
.
Thus, there exist
u_{1}\in V(xTw)\cap V(T_{1})\setminus{\sf Im}(f)
and
u_{2}\in V(xTw)\cap V(T_{2})\setminus{\sf Im}(f)
.
Without loss of generality, suppose that
x\in V(T_{1})
.
We take such a node
u_{2}
that is closest to
x
and
u_{1}
to be the node just before
u_{2}
in the path from
x
to
u_{2}
.
Since
V(xTu_{1})\subseteq V(T_{1})\setminus{\sf Im}(f)
and
u_{2}\in V(T_{2})\setminus{\sf Im}(f)
,
\chi(u_{2})
and
\chi(xTu_{1})
are not connected.
This contradicts the fact that
G_{v}[\chi(T_{u_{2}})]
is connected.
Therefore,
\hat{T}\in{\sf join}^{*}(\hat{T}_{1},\hat{T}_{2})
, which implies that
\hat{T}\in{\sf join}^{*}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
.

Join procedure.

Given two annotated trees
\hat{T}_{1}
and
\hat{T}_{2}
, the
join procedure
, denoted by
{\sf join}(\hat{T}_{1},\hat{T}_{2})
, outputs
{\sf filter}_{k}\circ{\sf rep}\circ{\sf join}^{*}(\hat{T}_{1},\hat{T}_{2})
.
See
Figure 8
for an example.
Note that we do not apply the crop operation since joining two cropped annotated trees gives a cropped annotated tree.
Given two sets
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
of annotated trees,
{\sf join}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
outputs
\bigcup_{\hat{T}_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{1},\hat{T}_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{2}}{\sf join}(\hat{T}_{1},\hat{T}_{2})
.

Figure 8:
An annotated tree (below) obtained from the join procedure applied to the two annotated trees above.

Lemma 7.21
.

Let
G
be a graph,
({\sf T},\beta,{\sf r})
be a nice tree decomposition of
G
,
v
be a join node of
{\sf T}
with children
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, and
\rho:\beta(v)\to[|\beta(v)|]
be a bijection.
Let
k
be an integer,
\mathcal{A}:={\sf char}_{k}(G_{v},\beta(v),\rho)
,
\mathcal{A}_{1}:={\sf char}_{k}(G_{v_{1}},\beta(v_{1}),\rho)
, and
\mathcal{A}_{2}:={\sf char}_{k}(G_{v_{2}},\beta(v_{2}),\rho)
.
Then
\mathcal{A}={\sf join}_{k}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
.

Proof 7.22
.

Let
\mathcal{B}:={\sf char^{*}}(G_{v},\beta(v),\rho)
,
\mathcal{B}_{1}:={\sf char^{*}}(G_{v_{1}},\beta(v_{1}),\rho)
, and
\mathcal{B}_{2}:={\sf char^{*}}(G_{v_{2}},\beta(v_{2}),\rho)
.
According to
Lemma 7.19
,
\mathcal{B}={\sf join}^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2})
.

Let
\hat{T}=(T,r,h,{\bf R},f)\in\mathcal{B}
,
\hat{T}_{1}=(T_{1},r_{1},h_{1},{\bf R}_{1},f_{1})\in\mathcal{B}_{1}
, and
\hat{T}_{2}=(T_{2},r_{2},h_{2},{\bf R}_{2},f_{2})\in\mathcal{B}_{2}
, such that
\hat{T}\in{\sf join}^{*}(\hat{T}_{1},\hat{T}_{2})
.
The complete join procedure joins
{\bf R}_{1}
and
{\bf R}_{2}
to obtain
{\bf R}
, without modifying their boundary nor deleting vertices or edges, so given two sets of annotated trees
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
,
{\sf rep}\circ{\sf join}^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2})={\sf rep}\circ{\sf join}^{*}({\sf rep}(\mathcal{C}_{1}),{\sf rep}(\mathcal{C}_{2}))
.

The procedure can only increase the height of annotated trees, so
{\sf filter}_{k}\circ{\sf join}^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2})={\sf filter}_{k}\circ{\sf join}^{*}({\sf filter}_{k}(\mathcal{C}_{1}),{\sf filter}_{k}(\mathcal{C}_{2}))
.

Moreover, item 6 of the
\diamondsuit_{\sf join}
operation implies that, if for
w\in V(T)
,
V(T_{w})\cap{\sf Im}(f)=\emptyset
, then there is
i\in\{1,2\}
such that
T_{w}=(T_{i})_{w}
.
Therefore, each cropped subtree of
T
is exactly a cropped subtree of
T_{1}
or a cropped subtree of
T_{2}
.
Thus,
{\sf crop}\circ{\sf join}^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2})={\sf join}^{*}({\sf crop}(\mathcal{C}_{1}),{\sf crop}(\mathcal{C}_{2}))
.

Since these three operations are commutative, we have
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{M}_{k}(\mathcal{B})={\sf filter}_{k}\circ{\sf rep}\circ{\sf join}^{*}(\mathcal{M}_{k}(\mathcal{B}_{1}),\mathcal{M}_{k}(\mathcal{B}_{2}))={\sf join}_{k}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
.

For the join procedure, we finally prove that it can generate a bounded number of annotated trees.

Lemma 7.23
.

Let
w
and
k
be two positive integers, let
{\bf G}_{1}
and
{\bf G}_{2}
be two compatible
w
-boundaried graphs, let
\hat{T}_{1}\in{\sf char}_{k}({\bf G}_{1})
, and let
\hat{T}_{2}\in{\sf char}_{k}({\bf G}_{2})
.
Then
|{\sf join}_{k}(\hat{T}_{1},\hat{T}_{2})|=2^{\mathcal{O}(w\cdot k)}
.

Proof 7.24
.

Let
\hat{T}_{1}=(T_{1},r_{1},h_{1},{\bf R}_{1},f_{1})
and
\hat{T}_{2}=(T_{2},r_{2},h_{2},{\bf R}_{2},f_{2})
.
We will first show that

|(T_{1},r_{1},f_{1})\diamondsuit_{\sf join}(T_{2},r_{2},f_{2})|=2^{\mathcal{O}(w\cdot k)}.

Let
(T,r,f)\in(T_{1},r_{1},f_{1})\diamondsuit_{\sf join}(T_{2},r_{2},f_{2})
.
Notice that
(T,r)
has at most
w
leaves and height at most
k
.
Each leaf of
(T,r)
is in
{\sf Im}(f)={\sf Im}(f_{1})={\sf Im}(f_{2})
, so it corresponds to both a leaf of
(T_{1},r_{1})
and a leaf of
(T_{2},r_{2})
.
Also note that
T
is obtained by choosing, for each path from a leaf
v
to
r
, a subset that corresponds to the path
vT_{1}r
(the rest is the path
vT_{2}r
).
There are at most
w
such paths from a leaf to
r
, and each of them has length at most
k+1
.
So
|(T_{1},r_{1},f_{1})\diamondsuit_{\sf join}(T_{2},r_{2},f_{2})|\leq 2^{w(k+1)}
.
Then, to construct an annotated tree
(T,r,h,{\bf R},f)
, the function
h
and the boundaried graph
{\bf R}
are totally
determined by
T
,
h_{1}
,
h_{2}
,
{\bf R}_{1}
, and
{\bf R}_{2}
.
So we obtain the desired result.

7.5
The algorithm

We finally present a recursive algorithm (
Algorithm 1
) that computes the elimination distance to
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
of a graph of bounded treewidth and proves
Theorem 7.1
. More precisely, given a boundary graph
{\bf G}
, a nice tree decomposition, and an integer
k
, the algorithm outputs
{\sf char}_{k}({\bf G})
.

1

Input:

A connected boundaried graph
{\bf G}=(G,X,\rho)
, a nice tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}=({\sf T},\beta,{\sf r})
of
G
, an integer
k
, and a node
v\in V({\sf T})
such that
X=\beta(v)
.

Output:

The characteristic
{\sf char}_{k}({\bf G})
of
\bf G
.

\mathcal{A}\leftarrow\emptyset

2

w\leftarrow|\beta(v)|

3

if

v
is a leaf

then

4

\mathcal{A}\leftarrow\{\bf{\mathds{1}}\}
else if

v
is a forget node with child
v^{\prime}
and forgotten vertex
x

then

5

\rho^{\prime}\leftarrow\rho\oplus[x\mapsto w+1]

6

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\leftarrow{\tt recEd}((G,\beta(v^{\prime}),\rho^{\prime}),\mathcal{T},k,v^{\prime})

7

\mathcal{A}\leftarrow{\sf forget}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})

else if

v
is an introduce node with child
v^{\prime}
and introduced vertex
x

then

8

\tau\leftarrow(x\leftrightarrow\rho^{-1}(w))

9

\rho^{\prime}\leftarrow\rho\circ\tau

10

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\leftarrow{\tt recEd}((G,\beta(v^{\prime}),\rho^{\prime}|_{\beta(v^{\prime})}),\mathcal{T},k,v^{\prime})

11

N\leftarrow N_{G[\beta(v)]}(x)

12

\mathcal{A}\leftarrow\tau({\sf intr}_{k}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}(N)))

else if

v
is a join node with children
v_{1}
and
v_{2}

then

13

\mathcal{A}_{1}\leftarrow{\tt recEd}((G,\beta(v),\rho),\mathcal{T},k,v_{1})

14

\mathcal{A}_{2}\leftarrow{\tt recEd}((G,\beta(v),\rho),\mathcal{T},k,v_{2})

15

\mathcal{A}\leftarrow{\sf join}_{k}(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})

return

\mathcal{A}

Algorithm 1

recEd
({\bf G},\mathcal{T},k,v)

Note that using backtracking in
Algorithm 1
, we can easily construct an annotated tree of minimum height in
{\sf char^{*}}({\bf G})
as well as its witness pair. In other words, given a connected graph
G
such that
{\sf ed}_{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}(G)\leq k
, we can construct an
\mathcal{F}
-elimination tree of
G
of height
{\sf ed}_{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}(G)
using
Algorithm 1
.

Lemma 7.25
.

Given a connected graph
G
, an integer
k
, a nice tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}=({\sf T},\beta,{\sf r})
of
G
of width
w
, a bijection
\rho:\beta({\sf r})\to[|\beta({\sf r})|]
, and
v\in V({\sf T})
,
recEd
((G,\beta(v),\rho),\mathcal{T},k,v)
outputs
{\sf char}_{k}(G_{v},\beta(v),\rho)
.
Moreover,
recEd
((G,\beta({\sf r}),\rho),\mathcal{T},k,{\sf r})
outputs
{\sf char}_{k}(G,\beta({\sf r}),\rho)
in time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(w\cdot k+w\log w)}\cdot n
.

Proof 7.26
.

We prove that for every
v\in V({\sf T})
, by induction on the height of
{\sf T}_{v}
,
the algorithm
recEd
of
Algorithm 1
with input
((G,\beta(v),\rho),\mathcal{T},k,v)
returns
{\sf char}_{k}(G_{v},\beta(v),\rho)
.
Indeed, if
v
is a leaf, then
{\sf char}_{k}(G_{v},\beta(v),\rho)=\{\hat{{\mathds{1}}}\}={\tt recEd}((G,\beta(v),\rho),\mathcal{T},k,v)
.
Otherwise,
v
is either a forget node, or an introduce node, or a join node, and the correctness of the algorithm is implied from the induction hypothesis and
Lemma 7.11
,
Lemma 7.15
, and
Lemma 7.21
, respectively.
Finally, since
G=G_{\sf r}
, we have
recEd
((G,\beta({\sf r}),\rho),\mathcal{T},k,{\sf r})={\sf char}_{k}(G,\beta({\sf r}),\rho)
.

We now analyze the running time.
A nice tree decomposition of width
w
constructed by
Proposition 4.10
has
\mathcal{O}(w\cdot n)
bags, hence the linear dependence follows.

Let us first analyze the join procedure.
During this procedure, we recursively obtain two characteristics of size
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(w\cdot k+w\log w)}
according to
Lemma 7.7
.
Each pair of annotated trees can be joined in
2^{\mathcal{O}(w\cdot k)}
ways, according to
Lemma 7.23
.
Let
{\bf R}_{1}
and
{\bf R}_{2}
be the boundaried graphs of such a pair of annotated trees. There is an integer
z\leq w
such that they belong to
\mathcal{R}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}^{z}
.
By
Proposition 4.11
,
{\bf R}_{1}
and
{\bf R}_{2}
have size
\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(z)
.
So
{\bf R}:={\bf R}_{1}\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.4}{$\oplus$}}}{\bf R}_{2}
has size
\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(z)
as well.
The representation operation applied to
{\bf R}
during the join operation for those two
annotated trees finds the representative of
l\leq z
boundaried graphs of respective boundaries of sizes
z_{1},...,z_{l}
with
\sum_{i=1}^{l}z_{i}\leq z
and with
\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(z_{i})
vertices in the underlying graph due to
Proposition 4.11
.
So by
Lemma 4.13
, the representation operation in the join procedure takes time
\sum_{i=1}^{l}2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(w_{i}\log w_{i})}=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(w\log w)}
.
Checking that this is an annotated tree, that its height is at most
d
, and that we did not already create it also takes time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(w\cdot k+w\log w)}
.
Hence, the total running time of the join procedure is
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(w\cdot k+w\log w)}
.

It is easy to see that the forget procedure applied to an annotated tree creates at most one annotated tree.
Moreover, the introduce procedure applied to an annotated tree creates
\mathcal{O}(w\cdot k)
annotated trees according to
Lemma 7.17
.
Similarly, the representation, crop, and filter operations in these procedures take time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(w\cdot k+w\log w)}
.
So the lemma follows.

We can finally prove
Theorem 7.1
.

Proof 7.27

(Proof of Theorem
7.1
)
.

If
\mathcal{F}=\{K_{1}\}
, the algorithm of
Proposition 7.4
outputs the desired result in time
2^{\mathcal{O}({\sf tw}\cdot k)}\cdot n
.
So let us assume that
\mathcal{F}
is non-trivial.

Suppose first that
G
is connected.
By
Proposition 4.5
and
Proposition 4.10
, we can obtain a nice tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}=({\sf T},\beta,{\sf r})
of width
2{\sf tw}+1
in time
2^{\mathcal{O}({\sf tw})}\cdot n
.
Let
\rho:\beta({\sf r})\to[|\beta({\sf r})|]
be an arbitrary ordering on the vertices of
\beta({\sf r})
.
We apply
recEd
with input
((G,\beta({\sf r}),\rho),\mathcal{T},k,{\sf r})
.
By
Lemma 7.25
, this gives the desired result in time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}({\sf tw}(k+\log{\sf tw}))}\cdot n
.

If
G
is not connected, we apply the same procedure on each connected component. The running time is the same as in the above case.

7.6
Exchangeability of boundaried graphs with the same characteristic

We give here a simple technical lemma on characteristics that will be used in
Section 10
.
We show that boundaried graphs with the same characteristic can be exchanged, i.e., give graphs of the same elimination distance to
\mathcal{F}
when “glued” to the same boundaried graph.

Given a positive integer
k
and a (possibly disconnected) boundaried graph
{\bf G}
, we define
{\sf char}_{k}({\bf G})
as
({\sf char}_{k}({\bf C}))_{{\bf C}\in{\sf cc}({\bf G})}
. Note that we still have
|{\sf char}_{k}({\bf G})|=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(w\cdot k+w\log w)}
. Therefore, we can extend
f

7.7

so that
|{\sf char}_{k}({\bf G})|\leq\hyperref@@ii[@caricatures]{f_{\ref*{@caricatures}}}(w,k)
with
\hyperref@@ii[@caricatures]{f_{\ref*{@caricatures}}}(w,k)=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(w\cdot k+w\log w)}
for any boundaried graph
{\bf G}
.

Lemma 7.28
.

Let
\bf G
,
\bf G^{\prime}
, and
\bf G^{\prime\prime}
be three compatible boundaried graphs and let
k
be an integer such that
{\sf ed}_{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}({\bf G}\oplus{\bf G^{\prime\prime}})\leq k
and
{\sf char}_{k}({\bf G})={\sf char}_{k}(\bf G^{\prime})
.
Then
{\sf ed}_{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}({\bf G}\oplus{\bf G^{\prime\prime}})={\sf ed}_{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}({\bf G^{\prime}}\oplus{\bf G^{\prime\prime}})
.

Proof 7.29
.

We suppose without loss of generality that
{\bf G}\oplus{\bf G^{\prime\prime}}
, and therefore
{\bf G^{\prime}}\oplus{\bf G^{\prime\prime}}
as well, is connected. Indeed, if this is not the case, we may apply the following proof to each one of the connected components separately.

Let
{\sf cc}({\bf G})=\{{\bf C}_{1},...,{\bf C}_{l}\}
and
{\sf cc}({\bf G^{\prime}})=\{{\bf C}_{1}^{\prime},...,{\bf C}_{l}^{\prime}\}
, such that
{\sf char}_{k}({\bf C}_{i})={\sf char}_{k}({\bf C}_{i}^{\prime})
for
i\in[l]
. Let
i\in[l]
. We write
{\bf C}_{i}=(C_{i},B_{i},\rho_{i})
.
Let
\mathcal{T}_{i}=({\sf T}_{i},\beta_{i},{\sf r}_{i})
be a nice tree decomposition of
{\bf C}_{i}
, i.e., such that
\beta_{i}({\sf r}_{i})=B_{i}
.
Since the
B_{i}
’s are pairwise disjoint, there is a rooted tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}^{*}=({\sf T}^{*},\beta^{*},{\sf r})
of
G^{\prime\prime}
, where
G^{\prime\prime}
is the underlying graph of
{\bf G^{\prime\prime}}
, such that, for
i\in[l]
, there is
v_{i}\in{\sf Leaf}({\sf T}^{*},{\sf r})
with
\beta^{*}(v_{i})=B_{i}
.
Let
\mathcal{T}=({\sf T},\beta,{\sf r})
be the tree decomposition obtained from
\mathcal{T}^{*}
and the
\mathcal{T}_{i}
’s by identifying
v_{i}
with
{\sf r}_{i}
for
i\in[l]
and adding nodes in
\mathcal{T}^{*}
using
Proposition 4.10
, so that
\mathcal{T}
is a nice tree decomposition of
{\bf G}\oplus{\bf G^{\prime\prime}}
.

Let
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=({\sf T}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},{\sf r})
be a nice tree decomposition of the graph
{\bf G^{\prime}}\oplus{\bf G^{\prime\prime}}
obtained from
\mathcal{T}
by replacing
\mathcal{T}_{i}
by a nice tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{i}^{\prime}=({\sf T}_{i}^{\prime},\beta_{i}^{\prime},{\sf r}_{i}^{\prime})
of
{\bf C}_{i}^{\prime}
for
i\in[l]
.
Observe that, for every
i\in[l]
, according to
Lemma 7.25
,
recEd
(({\bf G}\oplus{\bf G^{\prime\prime}},\beta(v_{i}),\rho_{v_{i}}),\mathcal{T},k,v_{i})={\sf char}_{k}({\bf G}\oplus{\bf G^{\prime\prime}},\beta(v_{i}),\rho)={\sf char}_{k}({\bf C}_{i})
.
Similarly,
recEd
(({\bf G^{\prime}}\oplus{\bf G^{\prime\prime}},\beta^{\prime}(v_{i}),\rho_{v_{i}}),\mathcal{T}^{\prime},k,v_{i})={\sf char}_{k}({\bf C}_{i}^{\prime})
.
Thus,
Algorithm 1
applied with input
(({\bf G}\oplus{\bf G^{\prime\prime}},\beta(v_{i}),\rho_{v_{i}}),\mathcal{T},k,v_{i})
and
(({\bf G^{\prime}}\oplus{\bf G^{\prime\prime}},\beta^{\prime}(v_{i}),\rho_{v_{i}}),\mathcal{T}^{\prime},k,v_{i})
outputs the same result.

We next set
U:=V({\sf T}\setminus\bigcup_{i\in[l]}V({\sf T}_{i}))
.
Note that
U=V({\sf T}^{\prime}\setminus\bigcup_{i\in[l]}V({\sf T}_{i}^{\prime}))
.
Therefore, in each node
u
of
U
,

Algorithm 1
applied with input
(({\bf G}\oplus{\bf G^{\prime\prime}},\beta(u),\rho_{u}),\mathcal{T},k,u)
and
(({\bf G^{\prime}}\oplus{\bf G^{\prime\prime}},\beta^{\prime}(u),\rho_{u}),\mathcal{T}^{\prime},k,u)
outputs the same result.
Thus,
Algorithm 1
applied with input
(({\bf G}\oplus{\bf G^{\prime\prime}},\beta({\sf r}),\rho_{\sf r}),\mathcal{T},k,{\sf r})
and
(({\bf G^{\prime}}\oplus{\bf G^{\prime\prime}},\beta^{\prime}({\sf r}),\rho_{\sf r}),\mathcal{T}^{\prime},k,{\sf r})
outputs the same result.
So
{\sf char}_{k}({\bf G}\oplus{\bf G^{\prime\prime}},\beta({\sf r}),\rho_{\sf r})={\sf char}_{k}({\bf G^{\prime}}\oplus{\bf G^{\prime\prime}},\beta^{\prime}({\sf r}),\rho_{\sf r})
.
Therefore, according to
Lemma 7.5
,
{\sf ed}_{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}({\bf G}\oplus{\bf G^{\prime\prime}})={\sf ed}_{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}({\bf G^{\prime}}\oplus{\bf G^{\prime\prime}})
.

8
Elimination distance to a minor-closed graph class

We finally present our main result for
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
. The following theorem is a restatement of
Theorem 1.2
.

Theorem 8.1
.

For every finite collection of graphs
\mathcal{F}
, there exists an algorithm that, given a graph
G
and an integer
k
,
decides whether
{\sf ed}_{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}(G)\leq k
in time
2^{2^{2^{k^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(1)}}}}\cdot n^{2}
.
In the particular case when
\mathcal{F}
contains an apex-graph, this algorithm runs in time
2^{2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2}\log k)}}\cdot n^{2}
.

This algorithm is very similar to the one in
Section 6
.
We use the same propositions and lemmata, except from the ones that were tailored for
\mathcal{F}
-M-Deletion
and for which we need to find an analogous result for
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
.
In
Section 7
, we described an algorithm to find the elimination distance when the treewidth is bounded, which is the analogue of
Proposition 3.2
for
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
. In
Subsection 8.1
, we present an analogue of
Proposition 3.1
, which either reports an upper bound on the treewidth of the input graph, or finds a wall, or reports that we deal with a
no
-instance.

In contrast to the algorithm for
\mathcal{F}
-M-Deletion
, we cannot use branching the same way because we do not have a bound in
k
on the number of vertices in a
k
-elimination set, like we had for a
k
-apex set. Thus, while Step 3 of
Section 6
could be applied at most
a_{\mathcal{F}}^{k}
times, such a Step 3 would now be applied at most
a_{\mathcal{F}}^{n}
times, which is not efficient.
Since we do not use branching anymore to reduce the size of the apex set of a flatness pair, when we apply
Proposition 3.5
, the size
a
of the apex set in the input depends on
k
. Hence, we need to find in the beginning a wall whose size dependence in
k
is triple-exponential.
This explains the triple-exponential in
k
that appears in the running time of
Theorem 8.1
.
The above algorithm is described in
Subsection 8.2
and in
Subsection 8.3
we present the proof of its correctness.

8.1
Quickly finding a wall

We first prove
Proposition 3.1
in the case of
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
.
The proof is very similar to the one given in
[
70
]
for
Proposition 3.1
in the case of
\mathcal{F}
-M-Deletion
and is achieved using the following result from Perkovic and Reed
[
59
]
. The main difference with respect to the proof of
Proposition 3.1
given in
[
70
]
is that we need to use two new ingredients tailored for
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
, namely
Theorem 7.1
and
Lemma 4.8
.

Proposition 8.2

(
[
59
]
)
.

There exists an algorithm with the following specifications:

Input
: A graph
G
and
t\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
|V(G)|\geq 12t^{3}
.

Output
: A graph
G^{*}
such that
|V(G^{*})|\leq(1-\frac{1}{16t^{2}})\cdot|V(G)|
and,

•

either
G^{*}
is a subgraph of
G
such that
{\sf tw}(G)={\sf tw}({G^{*}})
, or

•

G^{*}
is obtained from
G
after contracting the edges of a matching in
G
.

Moreover, the algorithm runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(t)}\cdot n
.

Proof 8.3

(Proof of Proposition
3.1
in the case of
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
)
.

.ddd

Let
c:=\hyperref@@ii[@relentlessly]{f_{\ref*{@relentlessly}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}})\cdot r+k
.

Suppose that
|V(G)|<12c^{3}
.
Run the algorithm of
[
8
]
that, in time
\mathcal{O}(|V(G)|^{c+2})=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}((r+k)\cdot\log(r+k))}
, checks whether
{{\sf tw}}(G)\leq c
. If this is the case, report the same and stop.
If not, we aim to find an
r
-wall of
G
or conclude that we are dealing with a
no
-instance.
First consider an arbitrary ordering
(v_{1},\dots,v_{|V(G)|})
of the vertices of
G
.
For each
i\in[|V(G)|]
, set
G_{i}
to be the graph induced by the vertices
v_{1},\dots,v_{i}
.
Iteratively run the algorithm of
Proposition 4.5
on
G_{i}
and
c
for increasing values of
i
.
This algorithm runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(c)}\cdot|V(G)|=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(r+k)}
.
Let
j\in[|V(G)|]
be the smallest integer such that the above algorithm outputs a report that
{{\sf tw}}(G_{j})>c
(it exists since
{{\sf tw}}(G)>c
) and notice that there exists a tree decomposition
({\sf T}_{j},\beta_{j})
of
G_{j}
of width at most
2c+2
, obtained by the one of
G_{j-1}
by adding the vertex
v_{j}
to all the bags.
Thus, we can call the algorithm of
Theorem 7.1
with input
(G_{j},2c+2,k)
, which runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(c\cdot(k+\log c))}\cdot|V(G_{j})|=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}((r+k)\cdot(k+\log(r+k)))}
, in order to find, if it exists, a
k
-elimination set
S_{j}
of
G_{j}
for
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
.

\bullet

If such a set
S_{j}
does not exist, then safely report that
(G,k)
is a
no
-instance.

\bullet

If such a set
S_{j}
exists, then call the algorithm of
Proposition 4.7
for
G_{j}\setminus S_{j}
, (and the decomposition of
G_{j}\setminus S_{j}
obtained from
({\sf T}_{j},\beta_{j})
by removing the vertices of
S_{j}
from all the bags) in order to check whether it contains an elementary
r
-wall
W
as a minor.
This algorithm runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(c\cdot\log c)}\cdot r^{\mathcal{O}(c)}\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(r^{2})}\cdot|V(G_{j}\setminus S_{j})|=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}((r+k)\cdot\log(r+k))}\cdot r^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(r+k)}\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(r^{2})}=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(r^{2}+(r+k)\cdot\log(r+k))}
, since
|E(W)|=\mathcal{O}(r^{2})
.
Since all connected components of
G_{j}\setminus S_{j}
are in
{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}
,
G_{j}\setminus S_{j}
does not contain
K_{s_{\mathcal{F}}}
as a minor. By
Lemma 4.8
,
{\sf tw}(G_{j}\setminus S_{j})\geq c-k=\hyperref@@ii[@relentlessly]{f_{\ref*{@relentlessly}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}})\cdot r
. So because of
Proposition 5.1
, the algorithm of
Proposition 4.7
will output an elementary
r
-wall
W
of
G_{j}\setminus S_{j}
.
We also return
W
as a wall of
G
.

Therefore, in the case where
|V(G)|<12c^{3}
, we obtain one of the three possible outputs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(r^{2}+k^{2})}
.

If
|V(G)|\geq 12c^{3}
, then call the algorithm of
Proposition 8.2
with input
(G,c)
, which outputs a graph
G^{*}
such that
|V(G^{*})|\leq(1-\frac{1}{16c^{2}})\cdot|V(G)|
and

•

either
G^{*}
is a subgraph of
G
such that
{\sf tw}(G)={\sf tw}(G^{*})
, or

•

G^{*}
is obtained from
G
after contracting the edges of a matching in
G
.

In both cases, recursively call the algorithm on
G^{*}
and distinguish the following two cases.

Case 1:
G^{*}
is a subgraph of
G
such that
{\sf tw}(G)={\sf tw}(G^{*})
.

(a)

If the recursive call on
G^{*}
reports that
{\sf tw}(G^{*})\leq c
, then return that
{\sf tw}(G)\leq c
.

(b)

If the recursive call on
G^{*}
outputs an
r
-wall
W
of
G^{*}
, then return
W
as a wall of
G
.

(c)

If
(G^{*},k)
is a
no
-instance, then report that
(G,k)
is also a
no
-instance.

Case 2:
G^{*}
is obtained from
G
after contacting the edges of a matching in
G
.

(a)

If the recursive call on
G^{*}
reports that
{\sf tw}(G^{*})\leq c
, then do the following.
First notice that the fact that
{\sf tw}(G^{*})\leq c
implies that
{\sf tw}(G)\leq 2c
,
since we can obtain a tree decomposition
({\sf T},\beta)
of
G
from a tree decomposition
({\sf T}^{*},\beta^{*})
of
G^{*}
,
by replacing, in every
t\in V({\sf T}^{*})
, every occurrence of a vertex of
G^{*}
that is a result of an edge contraction by its endpoints in
G
.
Thus, we can call the algorithm of
Theorem 7.1
with input
(G,2c,k)
, which runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(c(k+\log c))}\cdot n
, in order to find, if it exists, a
k
-elimination set
S
of
G
for
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
. We distinguish again two cases.

\bullet

If such a set
S
does not exist, then the algorithm reports that
(G,k)
is a
no
-instance.

\bullet

If such a set
S
exists, then apply the algorithm of
Proposition 4.5
with input
(G\setminus S,2c)
, which runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(c)}\cdot n
, and obtain a tree decomposition of
G\setminus S
of width at most
4c+1
.
Using this decomposition, call the algorithm of
Proposition 4.7
for
G\setminus S
in order to check whether it contains an elementary
r
-wall
W
as a minor.
This algorithm runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(c\cdot\log c)}\cdot r^{\mathcal{O}(c)}\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(r^{2})}\cdot n=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}((r+k)\cdot\log(r+k))}\cdot r^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(r+k)}\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(r^{2})}\cdot n=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(r^{2}+(r+k)\cdot\log(r+k))}\cdot n
, since
|E(G\setminus S)|=\mathcal{O}(n)
and
|E(W)|=\mathcal{O}(r^{2})
.
If this algorithm outputs an elementary
r
-wall
W
of
G\setminus S
, then output
W
. Otherwise, safely report, because of
Proposition 5.1
and
Lemma 4.8
, that
{\sf tw}(G)\leq\hyperref@@ii[@relentlessly]{f_{\ref*{@relentlessly}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}})\cdot r+k=c
.

(b)

If the recursive call on
G^{*}
outputs an
r
-wall
W^{*}
of
G^{*}
, then by uncontracting the edges of
M
in
W^{*}
, we can return an
r
-wall of
G
.

(c)

If
(G^{*},k)
is a
no
-instance, then report that
(G,k)
is also a
no
-instance.

It is easy to see that the running time of the above algorithm is given by the function

T(n,k,r)\ \leq\ T\left((1-\frac{1}{16c^{2}})\cdot n,k,r\right)+2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(r^{2}+k^{2})}\cdot n,

which implies that
T(n,k,r)=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(r^{2}+k^{2})}\cdot n
, as claimed.

8.2
Description of the algorithm for
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance

We define the following constants.

\displaystyle a=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@collaboration]{f_{\ref*{@collaboration}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}}+k),

\displaystyle q=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@categories]{f_{\ref*{@categories}}}(a_{\mathcal{F}},s_{\mathcal{F}},k(k+1)/2),

\displaystyle p=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@provincial]{f_{\ref*{@provincial}}}(a_{\mathcal{F}},s_{\mathcal{F}},k(k+1)/2),

\displaystyle l=

\displaystyle\ (q-1)\cdot a,

\displaystyle d=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@deliberation]{f_{\ref*{@deliberation}}}(a_{\mathcal{F}}-1,\ell_{\mathcal{F}}),

\displaystyle r_{4}=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@differences]{f_{\ref*{@differences}}}(a_{\mathcal{F}}-1,\ell_{\mathcal{F}},3,k(k+1)/2),

\displaystyle r_{3}=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@philistines]{f_{\ref*{@philistines}}}(r_{4},a_{\mathcal{F}}-1,a,d),

\displaystyle r_{2}=

\displaystyle\ {\sf odd}(\max\{\hyperref@@ii[@idealistic]{f_{\ref*{@idealistic}}}(l+1,r_{3},p),\hyperref@@ii[@unaffected]{f_{\ref*{@unaffected}}}(a_{\mathcal{F}},s_{\mathcal{F}},k(k+1)/2)\}),

\displaystyle r_{1}=

\displaystyle\ {\sf odd}(\hyperref@@ii[@corollaries]{f_{\ref*{@corollaries}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}}+k)\cdot r_{2}),

Note that
r_{4}=\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2})
,
r_{3}=\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2\cdot c})
,
r_{2}=\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2\cdot c+15})
, and
r_{1}=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2}\log k+c\log k)}
, where
c=\hyperref@@ii[@withdrawing]{f_{\ref*{@withdrawing}}}(a_{\mathcal{F}}-1,a,d)=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{24\cdot(a_{\mathcal{F}}-1)})}
.
Recall from
Subsection 2.2
that we assume that
G
has
\mathcal{O}_{s_{\mathcal{F}}}(k\sqrt{\log k}\cdot n)
edges.

Run the algorithm
Find-Wall-Ed
from
Proposition 3.1
with input
(G,r_{1},k)

and, in time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(r_{1}^{2}+k^{2})}\cdot n=2^{2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2}\log k+c\log k)}}\cdot n
,

•

either report a
no
-instance, or

•

conclude that
{\sf tw}(G)\leq\hyperref@@ii[@veneration]{f_{\ref*{@veneration}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}})\cdot r_{1}+k
and solve
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
in time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}((r_{1}+k)k+(r_{1}+k)\log(r_{1}+k))}\cdot n=2^{2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2}\log k+c\log k)}}\cdot n
using the algorithm of
Theorem 7.1
, or

•

obtain an
r_{1}
-wall
W_{1}
of
G
.

If the output of
Proposition 3.1
is a wall
W_{1}
,
then run the algorithm
Clique-or-twFlat
of
Proposition 3.4
with input
(G,r_{2},s_{\mathcal{F}}+k)
.
This takes time
2^{2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2}\log k)}\cdot r_{2}^{3}\log r_{2}}\cdot n=2^{2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2}\log k+c\log k)}}\cdot n
.
If the result is a set
A
of size at most
a
and a regular flatness pair
(W_{2},\mathfrak{R}_{2})
of
G\setminus A
of height
r_{2}
whose
\mathfrak{R}_{2}
-compass has treewidth at most
r_{1}
, then proceed, otherwise output a
no
-answer.

Compute a
W_{2}
-canonical partition
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
of
G\setminus A
.
Compute the set
B
of vertices of
A
that are adjacent to at least
q

p
-internal bags of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
.
As in
Proposition 5.4
, compute a collection
\mathcal{W}=\{W^{1},...,W^{l+1}\}
of
r_{3}
-subwalls of
W_{2}
such that for every
i\in[l+1]
,
\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}_{2}}(W^{i})
is a subgraph of
\bigcup\{Q\mid Q\text{ is a $p$-internal bag of }\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}\}
and for every
i,j\in[l+1]
, with
i\neq j
, there is no internal bag of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
that contains vertices of both
V(\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}_{2}}(W^{i}))
and
V(\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}_{2}}(W^{j}))
.
By the choice of
l
, there is an
i\in[l+1]
such that no vertex of
\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}_{2}}(W^{i})
is adjacent to a vertex of
A\setminus B
.

Run the algorithm from
Proposition 5.2
with input
(G\setminus B,W_{2},\mathfrak{R}_{2},W^{i})
to obtain a
W^{i}
-tilt
(W_{3},\mathfrak{R}_{3})
of
(W_{2},\mathfrak{R}_{2})
in time
\mathcal{O}_{s_{\mathcal{F}}}(k\sqrt{\log k}\cdot n)
.

As a next step, we apply the algorithm
Homogeneous
of
Proposition 3.5
with input
(r_{4},a_{\mathcal{F}}-1,a,d,r_{1},G,B,W_{3},\mathfrak{R}_{3})
, which, in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(c\cdot r_{4}\log r_{4}+r_{1}\log r_{1})}\cdot(n+m)=2^{2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2}\log k+c\log k)}}\cdot n
, outputs a flatness pair
(W_{4},\mathfrak{R}_{4})
of
G\setminus B
of height
r_{4}
that is
d
-homogeneous with respect to
\binom{B}{<a_{\mathcal{F}}}
and is a
W^{*}
-tilt of
(W_{3},\mathfrak{R}_{3})
for some subwall
W^{*}
of
W_{3}
.

Finally, apply the algorithm
Find-Irrelevant-Vertex
of
Proposition 3.6
with input
(k(k+1)/2,a_{\mathcal{F}}-1,G,B,W_{4},\mathfrak{R}_{4})
, which outputs, in time
\mathcal{O}_{s_{\mathcal{F}}}(k\sqrt{\log k}\cdot n)
, an irrelevant vertex
v
such that
(G,k)
and
(G\setminus v,k)

are equivalent instances of
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
.
Then the algorithm runs recursively on the equivalent instance
(G\setminus v,k)
.

Since each run takes time
2^{2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2}\log k+c\log k)}}\cdot n
and there are at most
n
runs, the algorithm indeed runs in time
2^{2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2}\log k+c\log k)}}\cdot n^{2}
.

Note that
c=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{24\cdot(a_{\mathcal{F}}-1)})}
, so if
\mathcal{F}
contains an apex-graph, i.e., if
a_{\mathcal{F}}=1
, then
c=\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(1)
.
Thus, the running time is
2^{2^{2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{24\cdot(a_{\mathcal{F}}-1)})}}}\cdot n^{2}
in the general case and
2^{2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2}\log k)}}\cdot n^{2}
in the case where
\mathcal{F}
contains an apex-graph.

8.3
Correctness of the algorithm

Let
(G,k)
be a
yes
-instance and let
S
be a
k
-elimination set of
G
for
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
.
By running
Proposition 3.1
with input
(G,r_{1},k)
, the algorithm should either get a report that
{\sf tw}(G)\leq\hyperref@@ii[@veneration]{f_{\ref*{@veneration}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}})\cdot r_{1}+k
or find an
r_{1}
-wall. The correctness of the former is obvious, so we will focus on the latter.

Let
W_{1}
be an
r_{1}
-wall of
G
.
According to
Lemma 4.3
,
K_{s_{\mathcal{F}}+k}
is not a minor of
G
.
Moreover, since
W_{1}
is a wall of
G
of height
r_{1}
,
{\sf tw}(G)\geq{\sf tw}(W_{1})\geq r_{1}\geq\hyperref@@ii[@corollaries]{f_{\ref*{@corollaries}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}}+k)\cdot r_{2}
.
Hence, if the algorithm runs
Clique-or-twFlat
of
Proposition 3.4
with input
(G,r_{2},s_{\mathcal{F}}+k)
, it should obtain a set
A
of size at most
a
and a regular flatness pair
(W_{2},\mathfrak{R}_{2})
of
G\setminus A
of height
r_{2}
whose
\mathfrak{R}_{2}
-compass has treewidth at most
r_{1}
.

As described in the algorithm, due to
Proposition 5.4
and the fact that
r_{2}\geq\hyperref@@ii[@idealistic]{f_{\ref*{@idealistic}}}(l+1,r_{3},p)
, there is an
r_{3}
-wall
W^{i}
that is a subwall of
W_{2}
such that no vertex of
\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}_{2}}(W^{i})
is adjacent to a vertex of
A\setminus B
, where
B
is the set of vertices of
A
adjacent to at least
q

p
-internal bags of a
W_{2}
-canonical partition
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
of
G\setminus A
.

When the algorithm applies
Proposition 5.2
with input
(G\setminus B,W_{2},\mathfrak{R}_{2},W^{i})
, it obtains a
W^{i}
-tilt
(W_{3},\mathfrak{R}_{3})
of
(W_{2},\mathfrak{R}_{2})
. Due to
Subsection 5.3
and
Subsection 5.3
,
(W_{3},\mathfrak{R}_{3})
is a regular flatness pair of
G\setminus B
whose
\mathfrak{R}_{5}
-compass has treewidth at most
r_{1}
.
Thus, since
r_{3}=\hyperref@@ii[@philistines]{f_{\ref*{@philistines}}}(r_{4},a_{\mathcal{F}}-1,a,d)
, the algorithm can apply
Homogeneous
of
Proposition 3.5
with input
(r_{4},a_{\mathcal{F}}-1,a,d,r_{1},G,B,W_{3},\mathfrak{R}_{3})
to obtain a flatness pair
(W_{4},\mathfrak{R}_{4})
of
G\setminus B
of height
r_{4}
that is
d
-homogeneous with respect to
\binom{B}{<a_{\mathcal{F}}}
and is a
W^{*}
-tilt of
(W_{3},\mathfrak{R}_{3})
for some subwall
W^{*}
of
W_{3}
. According to
Subsection 5.3
,
(W_{4},\mathfrak{R}_{4})
is regular.

Let
S^{\prime}
be a
k
-elimination set of
G
for
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
.

Lemma 5.7
implies that there is a set
X_{S^{\prime}}\supseteq S^{\prime}
such that
G\setminus X_{S^{\prime}}\in{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}
and
{\sf bid}_{G\setminus A,W_{2}}(X_{S^{\prime}})\leq k(k+1)/2
.

Since
r_{2}\geq\hyperref@@ii[@unaffected]{f_{\ref*{@unaffected}}}(a_{\mathcal{F}},s_{\mathcal{F}},k(k+1)/2)
, every subset of
B
of size
a_{\mathcal{F}}
intersects
X_{S^{\prime}}
according to
Proposition 3.7
.
Hence,
|B\setminus X_{S^{\prime}}|\leq a_{\mathcal{F}}-1
.

Moreover, note that
(W_{3},\mathfrak{R}_{3})
is a
W^{i}
-tilt of
(W_{2},\mathfrak{R}_{2})
,
(W_{4},\mathfrak{R}_{4})
is a
W^{*}
-tilt of
(W_{3},\mathfrak{R}_{3})
, and
(W_{4},\mathfrak{R}_{4})
is a flatness pair of
G\setminus B
with
B\subseteq A
.
Thus, given a
W_{4}
-canonical partition
Q_{1}
of
G\setminus B
, there is a
W_{2}
-canonical partition
Q_{2}
of
G\setminus A
such that each internal bag of
Q_{1}
is an internal bag of
Q_{2}
.
Thus,
{\sf bid}_{G\setminus B,W_{4}}(X_{S^{\prime}})\leq{\sf bid}_{G\setminus A,W_{2}}(X_{S^{\prime}})
.

Hence, the algorithm can apply
Find-Irrelevant-Vertex
of
Proposition 3.6
with input
(k(k+1)/2,a_{\mathcal{F}}-1,G,B,W_{4},\mathfrak{R}_{4})
and obtain a vertex
v
such that, for any
k
-elimination set
S^{\prime}
of
G
for
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
,
G\setminus X_{S^{\prime}}\in{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}
if and only if
G\setminus(X_{S^{\prime}}\setminus v)\in{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}
.
Thus, there is a
k
-elimination set of
G
for
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
if and only if there is a
k
-elimination set of
G\setminus v
for
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
.
It follows that
(G,k)
and
(G\setminus v,k)
are equivalent instances of
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
.

Suppose now that
(G,k)
is a
no
-instance.
Note that as long as
Proposition 3.4
outputs a flatness pair
(W_{2},\mathfrak{R}_{2})
, what follows in the proof of correctness works even if
(G,k)
is a
no
-instance. Therefore, we will find an irrelevant vertex.
Otherwise, we would have declared a
no
-instance beforehand.
Thus,
Theorem 8.1
follows.

9
Elimination distance when excluding an apex-graph

In the case where
\mathcal{F}
contains an apex-graph, we obtain an alternative algorithm whose complexity is single-exponential in
k
and cubic in
n
. The following theorem is a restatement of
Theorem 1.3
.

Theorem 9.1
.

For every finite collection of graphs
\mathcal{F}
that contains an apex-graph, there exists an algorithm that, given a graph
G
and an integer
k
,
decides whether
{\sf ed}_{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}(G)\leq k
in time
2^{k^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(1)}}\cdot n^{3}
.

Contrary to the previous section, since
a_{\mathcal{F}}=1
, any vertex fulfilling the criteria of
Proposition 3.7
belongs to every
k
-elimination set
S
of the input graph for
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
.
In the case where
\mathcal{F}
contains an apex-graph in

[
70
]
, a Step 3 similar to the one of
Section 6
can be applied, where a vertex belonging to every
k
-apex set can be found. Hence, after running Step 3
k
times, a
k
-apex set is found and the algorithm stops.
However, a
k
-elimination set may have size
\Omega(n)
, so we may run Step 3
\Omega(n)
times.
Since our Step 3 below runs in quadratic time, this gives the cubic dependence of this algorithm.
Fortunately, since we apply Step 3, just like in
Section 6
and contrary to
Section 8
, we manage to find a flatness pair along with an apex set whose size does not depend on
k
. Hence, when applying
Proposition 3.5
, we do not get a triple-exponential dependence on
k
anymore for the size of the wall we need to find originally.

In order to remember the vertices that are found to belong to every
k
-elimination set, since they do not decrease
k
like in
Section 6
, we need to distinguish them in the input. Hence, we actually give here an algorithm to solve a more general problem with annotations described in
Subsection 9.1
.

9.1
Generalization to annotated elimination distance

Contrary to the previous section, since
a_{\mathcal{F}}=1
, when applying
Proposition 3.7
, we find a vertex that belongs to every
k
-elimination set
S
. Such vertices are taken into account by considering the following generalization of
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
.

Annotated
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance

Input:
A graph
G
, a set
S_{0}\subseteq V(G)
, and a non-negative integer
k
.

Objective:
Find, if it exists, a
k
-elimination set
S
of
G
for the class
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
such that
S_{0}\subseteq S
.

S_{0}
is a set of annotated vertices that corresponds to the vertices identified as vertices of every
k
-elimination set
S
while running the algorithm.
Clearly,
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
is the particular case of
Annotated
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
when
S_{0}
is empty.

In the following lemma, we generalize
Theorem 7.1
to its “annotated” version.
More precisely, we present a simple trick to reduce the above problem to its “unannotated” version while not changing the treewidth of the input graph so much.

Lemma 9.2
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a finite collection of graphs.
There is an algorithm that, given a graph
G
, a set
S_{0}\subseteq V(G)
, and two integers
k
and
{\sf tw}
such that the treewidth of
G
is bounded by
{\sf tw}
, decides whether
(G,S_{0},k)
is a
yes
-instance of
Annotated
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
in time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}({\sf tw}\cdot k+{\sf tw}\log{\sf tw}))}\cdot n
.

Proof 9.3
.

Given a minor-closed graph class
\mathcal{G}
, let
{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{G})}:=\{G\mid\forall C\in{\sf cc}(G),C\in\mathcal{G}\}
.
Bulian and Dawar
[
16
]
showed that if
H\in{\sf obs}(\mathcal{G})
has
l
connected components, then each graph
\bar{H}
obtained from
H
by adding
l-1
edges to obtain a connected graph belongs to
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{G}))
.
Thus, let
H_{\mathcal{F}}\in{\sf obs}(\mathcal{C}{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}))
obtained in such a way. As said above,
H_{\mathcal{F}}
is connected.

Let
G
be a graph of treewidth at most
{\sf tw}
and
S_{0}
be a subset of
V(G)
.
Let
G^{\prime}
be a graph obtained from
G
by gluing a graph
H_{v}
isomorphic to
H_{\mathcal{F}}
to each vertex
v
of
S_{0}
, where
v
is identified with an arbitrarily chosen vertex of
H_{v}
.

Let us show that
(G,S_{0},k)
is a
yes
-instance of
Annotated
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
if and only if
(G^{\prime},k)
is a
yes
-instance of
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{C}{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})))
-M-Elimination distance
.
If
S_{0}=\emptyset
, the proof is trivial, so we suppose
S_{0}\neq\emptyset
.

Let
(F^{\prime},\chi^{\prime},R^{\prime})
be a
\mathcal{C}{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})})
-elimination forest of
G^{\prime}
of height at most
k
with associated
k
-elimination set
S^{\prime}
.
For each
v\in S_{0}
, the fact that
H_{v}\notin\mathcal{C}{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})})
implies that
V(H_{v})\cap S^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
.
Let
y\in V(F^{\prime})
be the least common ancestor of
\chi^{\prime-1}(H_{v})
in
F^{\prime}
.
Since
H_{v}
is connected, according to
Lemma 4.1
,
y
exists and belongs to
\chi^{\prime-1}(H_{v})
.
Moreover, since
V(H_{v})\cap S^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
,
y\in{\sf Int}(F^{\prime},R^{\prime})
.

Let
(F^{\prime\prime},\chi^{\prime\prime},R^{\prime\prime})
be the
\mathcal{F}
-elimination forest of
G
obtained from
(F^{\prime},\chi^{\prime},R^{\prime})
as follows.
For every
v\in S_{0}
and every
t\in\chi^{\prime-1}(H_{v}\setminus v)

if
t\in{\sf Int}(F^{\prime},R^{\prime})
, remove
t
from
F^{\prime\prime}
and add edges between the parent and the children of
t

and if
t\in{\sf Leaf}(F^{\prime},R^{\prime})
,
remove
H_{v}\setminus v
from
\chi^{\prime\prime}(t)
.
If
G[\chi^{\prime\prime}(t)]
is not connected, then we update
F^{\prime\prime}
by replacing
t
by
|{\sf cc}(G[\chi^{\prime\prime}(t)])|
nodes, each one associated with a connected component of
G[\chi^{\prime\prime}(t)]
.

Thus, we have an
\mathcal{F}
-elimination forest of
G
associated with a
k
-elimination set
S
with
S_{0}\subseteq S
and with height at most
k
, implying that
(G,S_{0},k)
is a
yes
-instance of
Annotated
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
.

Conversely, given an
\mathcal{F}
-elimination forest
(F,\chi,R)
of
G
of height at most
k
, and
S_{0}\subseteq S:=\chi({\sf Int}(F,R))
, we can obtain a
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{C}{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})))
-elimination forest
(F^{\prime},\chi^{\prime},R^{\prime})
of
G^{\prime}
of height at most
k
by adding to each node
\chi^{-1}(v)
for
v\in S_{0}\subseteq S
a leaf associated with
H_{v}\setminus v\in\mathcal{C}{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})})
. The height of
(F^{\prime},\chi^{\prime},R^{\prime})
is indeed still at most
k
.
Therefore,
(G,S_{0},k)
is a
yes
-instance for
Annotated
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
if and only if
(G^{\prime},k)
is a
yes
-instance for
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{C}{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})))
-M-Elimination distance
.

Thus, if we apply the algorithm of
Theorem 7.1
to
(G^{\prime},k)
to solve
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{C}{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})))
-M-Elimination distance
, we solve
Annotated
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
on instance
(G,S_{0},k)
in time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}({\sf tw}(G^{\prime})\cdot k+{\sf tw}(G^{\prime})\log{\sf tw}(G^{\prime})))}\cdot|V(G^{\prime})|
.

Given a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}=({\sf T},\beta)
of
G
of width
t
, we can obtain a tree decomposition
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
of
G^{\prime}
of width at most
t+|V(H_{\mathcal{F}})|
by adding a node
x_{v}
for each
v\in S_{0}
such that
\beta(v)=V(H_{v})
, adjacent to a node
y
of
T
such that
v\in\beta(y)
. Thus,
{\sf tw}(G^{\prime})\leq{\sf tw}(G)+|V(H_{\mathcal{F}})|={\sf tw}(G)+\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(1)
.
Moreover,
|V(G^{\prime})|=|V(G)|+(|V(H_{\mathcal{F}})|-1)\cdot|S_{0}|=\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(|V(G)|)
.
Therefore, we can solve
Annotated
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
on instance
(G,S_{0},k)
in time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}({\sf tw}\cdot k+{\sf tw}\log{\sf tw}))}\cdot n
, and the lemma follows.

9.2
Description of the algorithm for
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
when
a_{\mathcal{F}}=1

We now describe the algorithm to solve
Annotated
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
, and hence
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
, when
a_{\mathcal{F}}=1
, i.e., when
\mathcal{F}
contains an apex-graph. Note that, similarly to this algorithm, the one from
Section 8
in the general case can also very easily be generalized to its “annotated” version.
We stress that the reason for the better parametric dependence of this algorithm compared to the algorithm of

Theorem 1.2

is that we pursue homogeneous flat walls where homogeneity is asked for subsets of size
not
depending on
k
.

We define the following constants.

\displaystyle a=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@collaboration]{f_{\ref*{@collaboration}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}}),

\displaystyle q=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@categories]{f_{\ref*{@categories}}}(1,s_{\mathcal{F}},k(k+1)/2),

\displaystyle p=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@provincial]{f_{\ref*{@provincial}}}(1,s_{\mathcal{F}},k(k+1)/2),

\displaystyle l=

\displaystyle\ (q-1)\cdot(k+a),

\displaystyle d=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@deliberation]{f_{\ref*{@deliberation}}}(a,\ell_{\mathcal{F}})

\displaystyle r_{4}=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@differences]{f_{\ref*{@differences}}}(a,\ell_{\mathcal{F}},3,k(k+1)/2),

\displaystyle r_{3}=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@philistines]{f_{\ref*{@philistines}}}(r_{4},a,a,d),

\displaystyle t=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@corollaries]{f_{\ref*{@corollaries}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}})\cdot r_{3},

\displaystyle r_{2}=

\displaystyle\ {\sf odd}(t+3),

\displaystyle r_{2}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\ {\sf odd}(\max\{\hyperref@@ii[@unaffected]{f_{\ref*{@unaffected}}}(1,s_{\mathcal{F}},k(k+1)/2),\hyperref@@ii[@idealistic]{f_{\ref*{@idealistic}}}(l+1,r_{2},p)\}),

\displaystyle r_{1}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\ {\sf odd}(\hyperref@@ii[@classifications]{f_{\ref*{@classifications}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}})\cdot r_{2}^{\prime}),

\displaystyle r_{1}=

\displaystyle\ {\sf odd}(r_{1}^{\prime}+k).

Note that
r_{4}=\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2})
,
r_{3},r_{2}=\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2c})
, and
r_{2}^{\prime},r_{1}^{\prime},r_{1}=\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2c+7/2})
, where
c=\hyperref@@ii[@withdrawing]{f_{\ref*{@withdrawing}}}(a,a,d)=\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(1)
.
Recall from
Subsection 2.2
that we assume that
G
has
\mathcal{O}_{s_{\mathcal{F}}}(k\sqrt{\log k}\cdot n)
edges.

The input of this algorithm is a graph
G
, a set
S_{0}\subseteq V(G)
, and an integer
k
.

Step 1.

Run the algorithm
Find-Wall-Ed
from
Proposition 3.1
with input
(G\setminus S_{0},r_{1},k)

and, in time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(r_{1}^{2}+k^{2})}\cdot n=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{4c+7})}\cdot n
,

•

either report a
no
-instance, or

•

conclude that
{\sf tw}(G\setminus S_{0})\leq\hyperref@@ii[@veneration]{f_{\ref*{@veneration}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}})\cdot r_{1}+k
and solve
Annotated
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
with input
(G,S_{0},\hyperref@@ii[@veneration]{f_{\ref*{@veneration}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}})\cdot r_{1}+2k,k)
in time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}((r_{1}+k)\cdot k+(r_{1}+k)\log(r_{1}+k)))}\cdot n=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2c+9/2})}\cdot n
using the algorithm of
Lemma 9.2
, or

•

obtain an
r_{1}
-wall
W_{1}
of
G
.

If the output of
Proposition 3.1
is a wall
W_{1}
, consider all the
\binom{r_{1}}{r_{2}}^{2}=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2c}\log k)}

r_{2}
-subwalls of
W_{1}

and for each one of them, say
W_{2}
, let
W_{2}^{*}
be the central
(r_{2}-2)
-subwall of
W_{2}
and let
D_{W_{2}}
be the graph obtained from
G\setminus S_{0}
after removing the perimeter of
W_{2}
and taking the connected component containing
W_{2}^{*}
.
Run the algorithm
Clique-or-twFlat
of
Proposition 3.4
with input
(D_{W_{2}},r_{3},s_{\mathcal{F}})
.
This takes time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(r_{3}^{2})}\cdot n=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{4c})}\cdot n
.
If for one of these subwalls the result is a set
A
of size at most
a
and a regular flatness pair
(W_{3},\mathfrak{R}_{3})
of
D_{W_{2}}\setminus A
of height
r_{3}
whose
\mathfrak{R}_{3}
-compass has treewidth at most
t
, then we proceed to Step 2, otherwise proceed to Step 3.

Step 2.

We obtain a 7-tuple
\mathfrak{R}_{3}^{\prime}
by adding all vertices of
G\setminus(S_{0}\cup V({\sf Compass}_{\mathfrak{R}_{3}}(W_{3})))
to the set in the first coordinate of
\mathfrak{R}_{3}
, such that
(W_{3},\mathfrak{R}_{3}^{\prime})
is a regular flatness pair of
G\setminus(S_{0}\cup A)
.

We first apply the algorithm
Homogeneous
of
Proposition 3.5

with input
(r_{4},a,a,d,t,G\setminus S_{0},A,W_{3},\mathfrak{R}_{3}^{\prime})
, which outputs, in time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(r_{4}\log r_{4}+t\log t)}\cdot(n+m)=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2c}\log k)}\cdot n
a flatness pair
(W_{4},\mathfrak{R}_{4})
of
G\setminus(S_{0}\cup A)
of height
r_{4}
that is
d
-homogeneous with respect to
2^{A}
and is a
W^{*}
-tilt of
(W_{3},\mathfrak{R}_{3}^{\prime})
for some subwall
W^{\prime}
of
W
.
At this point, we stress that the reason for the better parametric dependence of this algorithm compared to the previous one comes from the fact that the third input parameter
a
in
Homogeneous
does
not
depend of
k
.
We apply the algorithm
Find-Irrelevant-Vertex
of
Proposition 3.6

with input
(k(k+1)/2,a,G\setminus S_{0},A,W_{4},\mathfrak{R}_{4})
, which outputs, in time
\mathcal{O}_{s_{\mathcal{F}}}(k\sqrt{\log k}\cdot n)
, a vertex
v
such that
(G,S_{0},k)
and
(G\setminus v,S_{0},k)

are equivalent instances of
Annotated
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
.
Then the algorithm runs recursively on the equivalent instance
(G\setminus v,S_{0},k)
.

Step 3.

Consider all the
r_{2}^{\prime}
-subwalls of
W_{1}
, which are
\binom{r_{1}}{r_{2}^{\prime}}^{2}=2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2c+7/2}\log k)}
many,
and for each of them, say
W_{2}^{\prime}
,
compute its canonical partition
\mathcal{Q}
.
Then, contract each bag
Q
of
\mathcal{Q}
to a single vertex
v_{Q}
, remove the vertices
v_{Q}
where
Q
is not a
p
-internal bag of
\mathcal{Q}
, and add a new vertex
v_{\rm all}
and make it adjacent to all remaining
v_{Q}
’s. In the resulting graph
G^{\prime}
, for every vertex
y
of
(G\setminus S_{0})\setminus V(W_{2}^{\prime})
, check, in time
\mathcal{O}(q\cdot m)=\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{7}\sqrt{\log k}\cdot n)
, using a flow augmentation algorithm
[
20
]
, whether there are
q
internally vertex-disjoint paths from
v_{\rm all}
to
y
.
Let
\tilde{A}
be the set of such
y
’s.

If
\tilde{A}=\emptyset
, then report a
no
-instance.

If
1\leq|\tilde{A}|\leq k+a
, then each vertex of
\tilde{A}
should intersect every
k
-elimination set
S
of
G
for
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
.
The algorithm runs recursively on
(G,S_{0}\cup\tilde{A},k)
.

If, for every wall,
|\tilde{A}|>k+a
, then report that
(G,S_{0},k)
is a
no
-instance of
Annotated
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
.

After Step 2, the size of
G
decreases by one, so Step 2 can be applied at most
n
times.
After Step 3, the size of
S_{0}
increases by at least one, so Step 3 can also be applied at most
n
times.
Note that, if
S_{0}=V(G)
, then
{\sf tw}(G\setminus S_{0})=0
, so the algorithm stops.
Thus, the algorithm finishes.
Notice also that Step 3, when applied, takes time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2c+7/2}\log k)}\cdot n^{2}
, because we apply a flow algorithm for each of the
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2c+7/2}\log k)}

r_{2}^{\prime}
-subwalls and for each vertex of
G
.
Since Step 1 and Step 2 run in time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{4c+7})}\cdot n
and
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2c}\log k)}\cdot n
, respectively, and both may be applied at most
n
times, the claimed time complexity follows: the algorithm runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{4c+7})}\cdot n^{3}
.

9.3
Correctness of the algorithm

Let
(G,S_{0},k)
be a
yes
-instance and let
S
be a
k
-elimination set of
G
for
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
with
S_{0}\subseteq S
.
By running
Proposition 3.1
with input
(G\setminus S_{0},r_{1},k)
, the algorithm should either get a report that
{\sf tw}(G\setminus S_{0})\leq\hyperref@@ii[@veneration]{f_{\ref*{@veneration}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}})\cdot r_{1}+k
or find an
r_{1}
-wall.

If
{\sf tw}(G\setminus S_{0})\leq\hyperref@@ii[@veneration]{f_{\ref*{@veneration}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}})\cdot r_{1}+k
, then since
S_{0}\subseteq S
,
{\sf tw}(G\setminus S)\leq\hyperref@@ii[@veneration]{f_{\ref*{@veneration}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}})\cdot r_{1}+k
.
Hence, according to
Lemma 4.8
,
{\sf tw}(G)\leq\hyperref@@ii[@veneration]{f_{\ref*{@veneration}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}})\cdot r_{1}+2k
.

Otherwise, let
W_{1}
be an
r_{1}
-wall of
G\setminus S_{0}
.
According to
Lemma 5.5
, since
r_{1}\geq r_{1}^{\prime}+k
, there is an
r_{1}^{\prime}
-subwall
W_{1}^{\prime}
of
W_{1}
that is a subwall of
G\setminus S
.
Let
H
be the connected component of
G\setminus S
containing
W_{2}
.
The fact that
H
belongs to
{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}
implies that it has no
K_{s_{\mathcal{F}}}
-minor.
Therefore, by
Proposition 3.3
, since
r_{1}^{\prime}\geq\hyperref@@ii[@classifications]{f_{\ref*{@classifications}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}})\cdot r_{2}^{\prime}
, there is a set

B\subseteq V(H)
, with
|B|\leq a
,
and a flatness pair
(W_{2}^{\prime},\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\prime})
of
H\setminus B
of height
r_{2}^{\prime}
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the canonical partition of
W_{2}^{\prime}
.
Let
G^{\prime}
be the graph obtained after contracting every bag
Q
of
\mathcal{Q}
to a single vertex
v_{Q}
, removing the vertices
v_{Q}
where
Q
is not a
p
-internal bag of
\mathcal{Q}
, and adding a new vertex
v_{\rm all}
and making it adjacent to all remaining
v_{Q}
’s.
Let
\tilde{A}
be the set of vertices
y
of
G\setminus V(W_{2}^{\prime})
such that there are
q
internally vertex-disjoint paths from
v_{\rm all}
to
y
in
G^{\prime}
.
Since
S
is a
k
-elimination set of
G
for
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
,
there is a set
P\subseteq S
of size at most
k
so that
(L,R):=(V(G)\setminus V(H),V(H)\cup P)
is a separation of
G
with
P=L\cap R
.

Note that
\tilde{A}\subseteq P\cup B
.
To show this, we first prove that, for every
y\notin P\cup B
, the maximum number of internally vertex-disjoint paths from
v_{\sf all}
to
y
in
G^{\prime}
is
less than
q
.
Indeed,
if
y
is a vertex in
(V(G)\setminus V(H))\setminus P
, then every path from
y
to a vertex of
W_{2}^{\prime}
intersects
P
.
Therefore, there are at most
k<q
internally vertex-disjoint paths from
v_{\sf all}
to such a
y\in(V(G)\setminus V(H))\setminus P
in
G^{\prime}
.
If
y\in V(H)\setminus B
, then we distinguish two cases.
First, if
y
is a vertex in the
\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\prime}
-compass of
W_{2}^{\prime}
,
there are at most
k+a
such paths that intersect the set
P\cup B
and
at most four paths that do not intersect
P\cup B
(in the graph
G^{\prime}\setminus(P\cup B)
)
due to the flatness of
W_{2}^{\prime}
.
If
y
is in
V(H)
but not a vertex in the
\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\prime}
-compass of
W_{2}^{\prime}
, then, since by the definition of flatness pairs the perimeter of
W_{2}^{\prime}
together with the set
P\cup B
separate
y
from the
\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\prime}
-compass of
W_{2}^{\prime}
,
every collection of internally vertex-disjoint paths from
v_{\rm all}
to
y
in
G^{\prime}
should intersect the set
\{v_{Q_{\rm ext}}\}\cup P\cup B
, where
Q_{\rm ext}
is the external bag of
\mathcal{Q}
. Therefore, in all cases, if
y\notin P\cup B
, the maximum number of internally vertex-disjoint paths from
v_{\sf all}
to
y
in
G^{\prime}
is at most
k+a+4<q
.
Therefore,
y\notin\tilde{A}
.
Hence,
|\tilde{A}|\leq k+a
.

Let
\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
be the 7-tuple obtained by adding all vertices of
((G\setminus S_{0})\setminus P)\setminus H
to the set in the first coordinate of
\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\prime}
. Notice that since every path between
G\setminus H
and
H
intersects
P
,
(W_{2}^{\prime},\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\prime\prime})
is a flatness pair of
G\setminus(P\cup B)
.

If
\tilde{A}=\emptyset
, then let
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
be an enhancement of
\mathcal{Q}
on
G\setminus(P\cup B)
.
No vertex of
(P\cup B)\setminus S_{0}
is adjacent to vertices of at least
q

p
-internal bags of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
.
This means that the
p
-internal bags
of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
that contain vertices adjacent to some vertex of
P\cup B
are at most
(q-1)\cdot(k+a)=l
.

Consider a family
\mathcal{W}=\{W^{1},\ldots,W^{l+1}\}
of
l+1

r_{2}
-subwalls of
W_{2}^{\prime}
such that for every
i\in[l+1]
,
\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\prime\prime}}(W^{i})
is a subgraph of
\bigcup\{Q\mid Q\text{ is a $p$-internal bag of }\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}\}
and for every
i,j\in[l+1]
, with
i\neq j
, there is no internal bag of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
that contains vertices of both
V(\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\prime\prime}}(W^{i}))
and
V(\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\prime\prime}}(W^{j}))
. The existence of
\mathcal{W}
follows from
Proposition 5.4
and the fact that
r_{2}^{\prime}\geq\hyperref@@ii[@idealistic]{f_{\ref*{@idealistic}}}(l+1,r_{2},p)
.

The fact that the
p
-internal bags
of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}
that contain vertices adjacent to some vertex of
(P\cup B)\setminus S_{0}
are at most
l
implies that
there exists an
i\in[l+1]
such that
no vertex of
V(\bigcup{\sf influence}_{\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\prime\prime}}({W^{i}}))
is adjacent, in
G
, to a vertex in
(P\cup B)\setminus S_{0}
.

Let
W_{2}:=W^{i}
, let
W_{2}^{*}
be the central
(r_{2}-2)
-subwall of
W_{2}
, and let
D_{W_{2}}
be the graph obtained from
G\setminus S_{0}
after removing the perimeter of
W_{2}
and taking the connected component containing
W_{2}^{*}
.
Any path going from a vertex in
H
to a vertex in
G\setminus H
intersects
P
.
Thus,
D_{W_{2}}\subseteq H
and therefore,
K_{s_{\mathcal{F}}}
is not a minor of
D_{W_{2}}
.
Moreover,
W_{2}^{*}
is a wall of
D_{W_{2}}
of height
r_{2}-2\geq t+1
, so
{\sf tw}(D_{W_{2}})>t=\hyperref@@ii[@corollaries]{f_{\ref*{@corollaries}}}(s_{\mathcal{F}})\cdot r_{3}
.
Therefore, since , if the algorithm runs
Clique-or-twFlat
of
Proposition 3.4
with input
(D_{W_{2}},r_{3},s_{\mathcal{F}})
, it should obtain a set
A
of size at most
a
and a regular flatness pair
(W_{3},\mathfrak{R}_{3})
of
D_{W_{2}}\setminus A
of height
r_{3}
whose
\mathfrak{R}_{3}
-compass has treewidth at most
t
.
Hence, the algorithm then runs Step 2.

If
\tilde{A}\neq\emptyset
, then recall that for every
y\in\tilde{A}
,
y
has
q
internally vertex-disjoint paths
P_{1},...,P_{q}
to different
p
-internal bags
Q_{1},...,Q_{q}
of
\mathcal{Q}
in
G
.
Hence, there is an enhancement
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{y}
of
\mathcal{Q}
on
G\setminus(P\cup B)
such that
P_{i}
belongs to the bag
\tilde{Q}_{i}
that extends
Q_{i}
for
i\in[q]
.
Therefore,
y
is adjacent to vertices of at least
q

p
-internal bags of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{y}
.
Let
S^{\prime}
be a
k
-elimination set of
G
for
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
.
According to
Lemma 5.7
, there is a set
X_{S^{\prime}}\subseteq V(G)
such that
G\setminus X_{S^{\prime}}\in{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
and
{\sf bid}_{G\setminus(P\cup B),W_{2}^{\prime}}(X_{S^{\prime}})\leq k(k+1)/2
. Therefore,
y\in X_{S^{\prime}}
due to
Proposition 3.7
and the fact that
r_{2}^{\prime}\geq\hyperref@@ii[@unaffected]{f_{\ref*{@unaffected}}}(1,s_{\mathcal{F}},k(k+1)/2)
.
Let
C_{S^{\prime}}:=G\setminus X_{S^{\prime}}
.
Recall that
y
is adjacent to
q>k(k+1)/2

p
-internal bags of
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{y}
.
However,
{\sf bid}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{y}}(X_{S^{\prime}})\leq{\sf bid}_{G\setminus(P\cup B),W_{2}^{\prime}}(X_{S^{\prime}})\leq k(k+1)/2
.
Therefore,
y
is adjacent to
C_{S^{\prime}}
, so
y\in S^{\prime}
.
Since for every
y\in\tilde{A}
, for every
k
-elimination set
S^{\prime}
, we have
y\in S^{\prime}
, it implies that
\tilde{A}
is included in every
k
-elimination set of
G
for
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
.
Hence, if the algorithm runs Step 3, it then recursively runs on the equivalent instance
(G,S_{0}\cup\tilde{A},k)
.

We do not suppose that
(G,k)
is a
yes
-instance anymore.
Let us show the correctness of Step 2.
Suppose that we obtained the wanted flatness pair
(W_{3},\mathfrak{R}_{3})
in Step 1.
We obtain a 7-tuple
\mathfrak{R}_{3}^{\prime}
by adding all vertices of
G\setminus(S_{0}\cup V({\sf Compass}_{\mathfrak{R}_{3}}(W_{3})))
to the set in the first coordinate of
\mathfrak{R}_{3}
. Since
(W_{3},\mathfrak{R}_{3})
is a regular flatness pair of
D_{W^{i}}\setminus A
whose
\mathfrak{R}_{3}
-compass has treewidth at most
t
and since the vertices added in
\mathfrak{R}_{3}^{\prime}
are only adjacent to the perimeter of
W^{i}
, it follows that
(W_{3},\mathfrak{R}_{3}^{\prime})
is a regular flatness pair of
G\setminus(S_{0}\cup A)
whose
\mathfrak{R}_{3}^{\prime}
-compass has treewidth at most
t
.

If the algorithm applies the algorithm
Homogeneous
of
Proposition 3.5
with
(r_{4},a,a,d,t,G\setminus S_{0},A,W_{3},\mathfrak{R}_{3}^{\prime})
as input, it obtains a flatness pair
(W_{4},\mathfrak{R}_{4})
of
G\setminus(S_{0}\cup A)
of height
r_{4}
that is
d
-homogeneous with respect to
2^{A}
and is a
W^{*}
-tilt of
(W_{3},\mathfrak{R}_{3}^{\prime})
for some subwall
W^{\prime}
of
W
.
According to
Subsection 5.3
,
(W_{4},\mathfrak{R}_{4})
is regular.

Lemma 5.7
implies that for every
k
-elimination set
S^{\prime}\supseteq S_{0}
, there is a set
X_{S^{\prime}}\supseteq S^{\prime}
with
{\sf bid}_{G\setminus(S_{0}\cup A),W_{4}}(X_{S^{\prime}})\leq k(k+1)/2
and
G\setminus X_{S^{\prime}}\in{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
.
We have that
|A\setminus X|\leq|A|\leq a
, so the algorithm can apply
Find-Irrelevant-Vertex
of
Proposition 3.6
with input
(k(k+1)/2,a,G\setminus S_{0},A,W_{4},\mathfrak{R}_{4})
to obtain a vertex
v
such that for every
k
-elimination set
S^{\prime}\supseteq S_{0}
,
G\setminus X_{S^{\prime}}\in{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}
if and only if
G\setminus(X_{S^{\prime}}\setminus v)\in{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}
.
It follows that
(G,S_{0},k)
and
(G\setminus v,S_{0},k)
are equivalent instances of
Annotated
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
.

Suppose now that
(G,S_{0},k)
is a
no
-instance.
In Step 1, the algorithm either reports a
no
-instance or finds a wall.
In the latter case, the algorithm either goes to Step 2 or to Step 3.
If it runs Step 2, the previous paragraph justifies that the algorithm finds a vertex
v
such that
(G\setminus v,S_{0},k)
is a
no
-instance.
If the algorithm runs Step 3, then it either reports a
no
-instance or recursively runs on the instance
(G\setminus y,S_{0}\cup\tilde{A},k)
.
If
(G\setminus y,S_{0}\cup\tilde{A},k)
is
yes
-instance, then so is
(G,k)
. Thus,
(G\setminus y,S_{0}\cup\tilde{A},k)
. is a
no
-instance.
Hence, the algorithm always report a
no
-instance.
Therefore,
Theorem 9.1
follows.

Constructing the elimination ordering.

Notice that the results of
Theorem 8.1
and
Theorem 9.1
solve the decision version of

Elimination Distance to
\mathcal{G}
. Using the dynamic programming algorithm of
Section 7
,
we may find a
k
-elimination set
X
certifying that

{\sf ed}_{\mathcal{G}}(G)\leq k
. One may further determine, from

X
, the way the elimination ordering is applied on the vertices of
X
as follows. Let
{\sf torso}(G,X)
be the
graph obtained from
G[X]
if, for every connected component
C
of
G-X
, we make adjacent all pairs of vertices
in
N_{G}(V(C))
in
G[X]
.
Then we know that
{\sf td}({\sf torso}(G,X))\leq k
and the required elimination ordering
is the same as the one for
{\sf torso}(G,X)
, which can be computed by the algorithm of
[
61
]
in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k^{2})}\cdot n
.

10
Bounding the obstructions of
\mathcal{E}_{k}({\sf exc}(\mathcal{F}))

In this section, we prove the following result that provides an upper bound on the size of the graphs in
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{E}_{k}({\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})))
. The following theorem is a reformulation of
Theorem 1.5
.

Theorem 10.1
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a non-empty finite collection of non-empty graphs and
k
be a positive integer.
Every graph in
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{E}_{k}({\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})))
has
2^{2^{2^{2^{k^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(1)}}}}}
vertices.
In the particular case when
\mathcal{F}
contains an apex-graph, every graph in
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{E}_{k}({\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})))
has
2^{2^{k^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(1)}}}
vertices.

Recall that when
\mathcal{F}=\{K_{1}\}={\sf obs}(\mathcal{G}_{\emptyset})
, it is known
[
22
]
that every graph in
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{E}_{k}(\mathcal{G}_{\emptyset}))
has at most
2^{2^{k-1}}
vertices.

Theorem 10.1
implies that one can construct an algorithm that receives as input
{\sf obs}({\sf exc}(\mathcal{F}))
and
k
, and outputs
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{E}_{k}({\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})))
. This is done by enumerating all graphs on at most
f(k)
vertices, where
f(k)
is the bound on the number of vertices given by
Theorem 10.1
, and filtering out those that are members of
\mathcal{E}_{k}({\sf exc}(\mathcal{F}))
and taking those that are minor-minimal in what is left.
The running time of the algorithm can be bounded by
\mathcal{O}(2^{2^{2^{2^{2^{k^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(1)}}}}}}\cdot n^{2})
in the general case and by
\mathcal{O}(2^{2^{2^{k^{\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(1)}}}}\cdot n^{2})
if
\mathcal{F}
contains an apex-graph.

Note that this brute-force algorithm can be used to solve
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
. Indeed, to solve
\mathcal{F}
-M-Elimination Distance
, we can compute
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{E}_{k}({\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})))
and then check whether there is a graph in
{\sf obs}(\mathcal{E}_{k}({\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})))
that is a minor of the input graph. Of course, this algorithm is much less efficient than the ones presented in the previous sections.

The rest of the section is structured as follows: in
Subsection 10.1
we bound the treewidth of a minor-minimal obstruction of
\mathcal{E}_{k}({\sf exc}(\mathcal{F}))
, while in
Subsection 10.2
we bound the size of a minor-minimal obstruction of
\mathcal{E}_{k}({\sf exc}(\mathcal{F}))
of small treewidth.
This immediately implies
Theorem 10.1
.

10.1
Bounding the treewidth of an obstruction

In this subsection we aim to prove an upper bound on the treewidth of a minor-minimal obstruction of
\mathcal{E}_{k}({\sf exc}(\mathcal{F}))
.

Lemma 10.2
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a finite collection of graphs.
There exists a function
f_{15}:{\mathbb{N}}^{3}\to{\mathbb{N}}
such that if
G\in{\sf obs}(\mathcal{E}_{k}({{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}))
,
then
{\sf tw}(G)\leq\hyperref@@ii[@deceptively]{f_{\ref*{@deceptively}}}(k,a_{\mathcal{F}},s_{\mathcal{F}})
.
Moreover,
\hyperref@@ii[@deceptively]{f_{\ref*{@deceptively}}}(k,a,s)=2^{\log(k\cdot c)\cdot 2^{k^{a-1}\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(s^{2}\log s)}}}
, where
a=a_{\mathcal{F}}
,
s=s_{\mathcal{F}}
, and
c
is a constant depending on
\ell_{\mathcal{F}}
.

Note that when
a_{\mathcal{F}}=1
,
\hyperref@@ii[@deceptively]{f_{\ref*{@deceptively}}}(k,1,s)=\mathcal{O}_{\ell_{\mathcal{F}}}(k^{2^{2^{\mathcal{O}(s^{2}\log s)}}})
.

Proof 10.3
.

For simplicity, we use
s,a
, and
\ell
instead of
s_{\cal F},a_{\cal F}
, and
\ell_{\cal F}
, respectively.
We set

\displaystyle b=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@collaboration]{f_{\ref*{@collaboration}}}(s)+k+1,

\displaystyle d=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@deliberation]{f_{\ref*{@deliberation}}}(a-1,\ell),

\displaystyle r_{4}=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@differences]{f_{\ref*{@differences}}}(a-1,\ell,3,k(k+1)/2),

\displaystyle r_{3}=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@philistines]{f_{\ref*{@philistines}}}(r_{4},a-1,b,d),

\displaystyle x=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@categories]{f_{\ref*{@categories}}}(a,s,k(k+1)/2),

\displaystyle p=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@provincial]{f_{\ref*{@provincial}}}(a,s,k(k+1)/2),

\displaystyle l=

\displaystyle\ (x-1)\cdot b,

\displaystyle r_{2}=

\displaystyle\ {\sf odd}(\max\{\hyperref@@ii[@idealistic]{f_{\ref*{@idealistic}}}(l+1,r_{3},p),\hyperref@@ii[@unaffected]{f_{\ref*{@unaffected}}}(a,s,k(k+1)/2)\}),

\displaystyle r_{1}=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@classifications]{f_{\ref*{@classifications}}}(s)\cdot r_{2},\mbox{ and }

\displaystyle w=

\displaystyle\ \hyperref@@ii[@relentlessly]{f_{\ref*{@relentlessly}}}(s)\cdot r_{1}+k+1.

It is easy to verify that
w=2^{\log(k\cdot c)\cdot 2^{k^{a-1}\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(s^{2}\log s)}}}
.

Suppose towards a contradiction that
{\sf tw}(G)>w
.
Since
G\in{\sf obs}(\mathcal{E}_{k}({{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}))
, for each
v\in V(G)
,
G\setminus v\in\mathcal{E}_{k}({{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})})
.
Therefore, there exists a
(k+1)
-elimination set
S
of
G
for
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
.
Thus, for each
C\in{\sf cc}(G\setminus S)
,
C\in{{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})}
.
According to
Lemma 4.8
,
{\sf tw}(G\setminus S)>w-k-1=\hyperref@@ii[@relentlessly]{f_{\ref*{@relentlessly}}}(s)\cdot r_{1}
, so there is
C\in{\sf cc}(G\setminus S)
, such that
{\sf tw}(C)>\hyperref@@ii[@relentlessly]{f_{\ref*{@relentlessly}}}(s)\cdot r_{1}
.
Moreover,
K_{s}
is not a minor of
C
.
Therefore, according to
Proposition 5.1
,
C
contains an
r_{1}
-wall
W_{1}
.

Since
r_{1}=\hyperref@@ii[@classifications]{f_{\ref*{@classifications}}}(s)\cdot r_{2}
, by
Proposition 3.3
, there is a set
A\subseteq V(C)
of size at most
\hyperref@@ii[@collaboration]{f_{\ref*{@collaboration}}}(s)
and a flatness pair
(W_{2},\mathfrak{R}_{2})
of
C\setminus A
of height
r_{2}
such that
W_{2}
is a tilt of a subwall of
W_{1}
.
Due to
Proposition 5.3
, there is a regular flatness pair
(W_{2}^{\prime},\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\prime})
of
C\setminus A
of height
r_{2}
.

Since
S
is a
(k+1)
-elimination set of
G
for
{\sf exc}(\mathcal{F})
and
C\in{\sf cc}(G\setminus S)
, there exists a set
P\subseteq S
